# Claude Code Session: ad250276-e370-4e83-a80c-679c13eafdb8

### User

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. **Primary request and intent**
   - **The game.** The user is designing and building "Anthro" (working title), an anthropological and biological evolution sim. Societies develop through condition-based discoveries rather than a tech tree. The player is a god who can't act directly: they shape nature and nudge, have a prophet/vessel, can shape individuals before birth, and can nudge people at life branch points. The game asks whether cultures would naturally discover things, and how varied isolated cultures would become (the founding question is about native cultures without colonization).
   - **Plan of record:** a solo developer plus AI, target summer 2027. The first milestone is a headless C# sim, then Unity 6.3 visuals (Synty and polyperfect assets).
   - **The user's core principle:** everything structural, organic and natural. No hard-coded events, schedules or global constants for valuations. Decisions emerge from traits, situation, beliefs, relationships and culture. World facts should be grounded in real data. The user strongly dislikes patch fixes and "hard codes," and wants honesty about what is hand-set.
   - **Approved design additions:**
     - Regional animal pools with migration
     - Animal fear
     - Physical hunting
     - A body and disease model with causal deaths
     - Neighbor bands
     - Courtship
     - Prestige
     - Real-scale world (20 km, 200 m ecology cells; nothing off-world later)
     - Culture norms
     - Households and social fabric (trust, theft, fights, raids, dynamic splits, solo leaving and returning)
     - Cooking skill and Hearth interest
     - Cold caches, pemmican, snowshoes, bone grease
     - Medicine/healing ideas (healing tile, healers, remedy rituals that may be superstition, childhood and maternal immunity, inherited per-disease resistance, novel diseases)
     - Couple separation (approved, not built)
     - Culture as open-ended customs plus core value dimensions (approved, not built)
     - Crowding reducible by institutions later
   - **Most recent explicit requests:** "ok yeah sure build cooking and preservation and hearth and all that", then "prepare for compact rq".

2. **Key technical concepts**
   - **Sim structure:** a C# netstandard2.1 / C# 9 sim library with no Unity references, a headless runner, and xunit tests. Deterministic PCG32 seeds. Hourly ticks with daily updates.
   - **Decisions:** utility-scored options plus Gaussian noise, then argmax (`Decider.Options`, `Choose`, `BestAlternative`, `ScoreOf`).
   - **Learned expectations:** string topics such as "Forage", "Hunt", "danger:<species>", "norm:valor/sharing/peace", "lean:winter". Social copying is weighted by charisma, prestige, sociability and openness, and children absorb beliefs from those they watch.
   - **Hunting:** party votes weighted by influence; individual valuation is gain + glory − fear × myShare × (InjuryCost × severity + LifeValue × lethal) − distance. Weapons have range and power; flight distance grows with fear; charges target 1–2 people.
   - **Ecology:** regional pools, Poisson arrivals, autumn migration, danger memory.
   - **Body model:**
     - Stomach plus fat reserves; nourishment (Satiety) = Reserve/3.
     - Buffered lactation; parents feed children within the household share.
     - Disease objects spread through real contact.
     - A harm ledger records the cause of death plus what weakened the person.
     - Immunity costs energy.
   - **Daylight and maturity:** daylight by season (sunrise/sunset); body maturity instead of age gates.
   - **Households:** food ownership, demand sharing, trust vs. fondness with decay, theft with severity and deterrence, fights, raids, discontent, dynamic splits and returns, orphan adoption.
   - **Hearth:** cooking gains, soft food, firewood fuel, preservation, storage levels, cold caches, snow and snowshoes.
   - **Foraging:** Holling type II yields; plant richness; foresight stockpiling based on remembering the worst winter.
   - **Distances:** all expressed in meters via `WorldMap.FromMeters`. `CellMeters = 200`, map size 100.

3. **Files and code sections** (root: `C:\Users\nars7\Downloads\Ideas and Thoughts\Anthro\`)
   - **docs/PLANNING.md**
     - The design doc with Decided/Proposed/Open markers.
     - Includes the approved build additions and the "nothing scheduled, nothing global" principle.
   - **docs/PROGRESS.md**
     - The detailed log of every step: overnight steps 1–3, mortality pass, real-scale world, daylight/maturity/household, social fabric, strife v2, cooking/hearth.
     - Its last section, "Cooking, hearth & preservation (built; state at compaction)", lists the experiment results and next steps.
   - **docs/discovery-web.json**
     - 25 skills, 106 stages: start-known stages, Cooking tile (cook.roast known, cook.hotstone, cook.boil, cook.grease, cook.oven, cook.bread, cook.pot), store.cold, dry.pemmican, hide.snowshoe.
     - **docs/build-spec.py** validates refs and cycles and injects the JSON plus **web-map.js** into **milestone-1-spec.html** (the artifact).
   - **Anthro.Sim/Simulation.cs** (partial class)
     - Contains: founding (couples get households, a few days of food per hearth, `KnitTogether`, `SeedCulture` with valor/sharing/peace), `Step` (Metabolize, decide, hour), `Daily`, `DailyBody` (harm ledger, cold via `Shelter.ComfortTemp(Band, p)`, huddling, infection, sickness course, `MaxHealth`), `Infection`, `Contact`, `SleepingTogether`, `Kill` with weakenedBy, births/conception, `Pair` (households, residence norm), neighbors (`MoveNeighbors`, `Outbreaks`, `RenewNeighbors`, `MatureBonds`), camp moving (`ConsiderMoving`, `LocalValue`, `StayPull`), `Crowding`, `Prospects`, `Ties` (top 8 bonds × kin weight × (0.5 + trust)), `RecordKill`, wolves, `TravelHours(cells, p)` with snow, `DailyNeed`, `Metabolize`.
     - YearStats fields: Gathered, Hunted, Eaten, Spoiled, Needed, Shared, CookingGain, FireOutDays, Refusals, Thefts, Fights, Raids, Valor, Sharing.
   - **Anthro.Sim/Social.cs**
     - Households: `NewHousehold`, `MoveInto`, `LeaveHousehold`, `Adopt`, `MoveFood`, `HomeOf`.
     - `DailyBonds` (daily adoption check, UpdateDiscontent, ConsiderRaid, bond and trust decay), `Strife`.
     - `ShareMeat`, `BlameFailedHunt`, `AskForFood` (once a day), `ConsiderTheft` (relationship-targeted, severity, deterrence).
     - `Fight(a, b, over, intensity)`, `ConsiderRaid`, `ConsiderSplitting` (households, viability `Needed = 2 + (1 − boldness) × 6`, strike-out), `Depart`, `ConsiderReturns` (after a year away).
   - **Anthro.Sim/Activities/**
     - `Decider.cs`: options including Eat, Cook, GatherWood, Preserve; the hunger formula uses home 0.7 + band 0.3; foresight; maturity gates; non-essentials scaled by (1 − hunger × 0.7).
     - `Hunt.cs`: individual valuation, `CanHunt` bends with desperation, KeepGoing until dark, ShareMeat.
     - `FieldActivities.cs`: Forage with Holling yield `handling = 1.1×(0.5+skill)×(1+0.3×interest)×age; rate = handling×stock/(stock+2)×Richness/0.8`; exploration; snacking held back for hungry dependents; GatherFlint.
     - `CampActivities.cs`: Eat (household store, asking, theft, kids first, elders get 20% less from raw food), Rest, Socialize (contact, pairing, ConsiderMoving when restless), Knap, Care, Play, WatchAndLearn (absorbs beliefs).
     - `HearthActivities.cs`: `Cuisine.Gain`, `MakesSoftFood`, `StorageLevel`, `DailyBurn`; Cook, GatherWood, Preserve.
     - `Weapons.cs`, `Activity.cs`. The Verb enum includes Eat, Cook, GatherWood, Preserve.
   - **Anthro.Sim/People/**
     - `Person.cs`: Stomach, Reserve, WinterLow, Satiety, Illness/Immunities, HarmLedger, ColdStress, Affinity, Trust, Home, LeftDay, LastAskDay, LastTheftThoughtDay, Discontent, Places[4] per season, Restless, HasHungryDependents.
     - `Band.cs`: FoodStore pools Meat/Plants/Stored/Cooked/Dried with `Take(amount, out rawMeat, out raw)` and `DailySpoil(temp, storageLevel, coldCaches)`; Households, FoodTotal, MeatTotal, ResidenceNorm.
     - `Household.cs`: Food, Fuel, SoftFoodUntil, Knows, DaysOfFood.
     - `Disease.cs`: Cold, Fever, Flux, WoundRot, BadMeat, LungSickness.
     - `Physiology.cs`: MaxHealth, Maturity, HuntCapacity, CraftCapacity, ImmuneStrength including cold stress.
     - `Shelter.cs`: Protection(band, person), with fire protection only when the family fire is lit.
     - `Expectations.cs`: Danger/Norm topics, Learn, LearnDanger, Absorb, Snapshot.
     - `Traits.cs`: enums with Interest.Healing, Interest.Hearth, Skill.Cooking.
   - **Anthro.Sim/World/**
     - `WorldGen.cs`: size 100. `PlantDensity` is currently **2.4** (from the last sed). Wood initialized.
     - `Cell.cs`: Wood/WoodCapacity (forest 40), Richness, SeasonFactor.
     - `WorldMap.cs`: CellMeters 200, FromMeters.
   - **Anthro.Sim/Ecology/**
     - `Species.cs`: physical properties and regional pools.
     - `EcologySystem.cs`: migration, fear, wolves.
   - **Anthro.Runner/Program.cs**: flags and debug views. **Anthro.Runner/WebLoader.cs**.
   - **Anthro.Tests/SimulationTests.cs**: 9 tests, last passing before the cooking build. They haven't been re-run since the cooking and PlantDensity changes.

4. **Errors and fixes** (major ones)
   - **Overhunting / year-0 massacre:** fixed with regional pools, fear, physical hunting, and learned danger with risk vs. reward. The user rejected special-casing mammoths and immigration from nowhere.
   - **Random death rolls:** replaced with causal physiology. The user asked for every death to have a reason.
   - **Couples not forming:** caused by founding demographics and yearly pairing; replaced with courtship through socializing.
   - **"Go home" rule and summer gathering:** the user flagged these as hard-coded. Replaced with individual valuations, votes, and neighbors' real visits.
   - **Performance:** ConsiderMoving and Socialize were hot spots. Fixed by making restlessness trigger moving, per-season place memory, and belief exchange once per conversation.
   - **Camp ping-pong:** fixed with season-aware memory, gain vs. effort, and exploration.
   - **Band swelling to 165:** fixed with closest-bond ties, fission, and calibrated neighbor bonds.
   - **Disease:** flux overtuned (604 deaths), recalibrated. A step-2 vs. step-3 count error (under-5 deaths had been excluded). Inverted death causes, addressed with lactation buffering, child feeding, huddling, endemic illness, the harm ledger, reserves, yield-based labor, gathering rate, and foresight.
   - **Social fabric:**
     - Theft keyed to the stomach caused crime waves; now keyed to reserves and rate-limited.
     - Return loops; fixed with time away and trust-weighted ties.
     - Departing families' children were treated as orphans and adopted.
     - Orphans were left alone in households; now checked daily.
     - The earlier raids and grave thefts turned out to be artifacts of the orphan bug.
   - **Build errors:**
     - A name clash between the `Cells` field and the `Cells()` method; renamed the method `FromMeters`.
     - Missing `System.Linq` / `Anthro.Sim.Core` usings.
     - Use of an unassigned `fed` variable.
     - Python patch assertion mismatches (fixed by reading the actual text).
     - Bash heredoc quoting failures; worked around by writing scripts to the scratchpad.
   - **Open:** a segmentation fault crashed one dotnet run at PlantDensity 2.4 (seed around 2). Not yet investigated.

5. **Problem solving**
   - **Current core issue:** a fragile food economy. Starvation is about 82–93% of attributed deaths, while real foragers die mostly of illness. Spring "hungry gap" famines.
   - **Diagnosis:** the land was 2–4× too poor compared with real temperate forager density (0.1–0.2 people/km² should give 40–80 people on 400 km²), so there was never surplus to preserve.
   - **Tech experiment at PlantDensity 0.8:** baseline 3/6 survived, preservation 3/6, cooking 5/6, everything 5/6.
   - **Density tests:** 1.6 gave 6/6 survived (pops 2–23; starvation 84%, illness 7%, cold 6%). 2.4 gave 5/6 plus one crash (pops 12–74).
   - **Analysis given to the user:**
     - The game parts (discovery, god, belief) aren't built.
     - The motive weights are hand-set.
     - Frequency limits and cutoffs remain.
     - There's been much quick tuning, so the sim is not calibrated.
     - Conflict comes only from food.
     - Worlds differ in texture, not trajectory.

### Claude (Assistant)

Ready to compact. Everything is saved to `docs/PROGRESS.md`, including a handoff section at the bottom with the current state, the experiment results, and next steps. Nothing is committed to git.

**Where things stand:**
- **Cooking, firewood, and preservation are built.**
  - Roasting works from the start, and the more advanced cooking and preservation methods turn on once a band knows them.
  - Fire and warmth now depend on each family keeping its own hearth supplied with wood.
  - Winter snow slows travel unless people have snowshoes.
  - The discovery web is now 25 skills and 106 stages.
- **The underlying cause of the famines:** the land was 2–4× poorer than real-world foraging land, so bands never had a surplus to preserve.
  - At that original setting, 3 of 6 test bands survived 80 years.
  - At double the plant food (1.6), all 6 survived.
  - At triple (2.4, the current setting), 5 survived, and band sizes ranged from 12 to 74.
- **One run crashed** at the 2.4 setting. I haven't reproduced it yet.

**Next, in order:**
1. Fix the crash.
2. Settle the land richness against the real-world target of 40–80 people sustained, then rerun the technology comparison.
3. Build the discovery system.
4. Calibrate against real-world data.

### Claude (Assistant)

Agreed. For the record, no one literally leaves the map now. Everyone who departs goes to a neighbor band that exists on the map. But those bands are placeholders: their people don't eat, get sick, or have children, and they're silently removed at 65. So moving there is effectively leaving the simulation, which is the problem you're pointing at, and it's why my death counts were so low.

**What I'd build to fix it:**
- **Every group is a real band.** Every band runs the same model: food, bodies, disease, households, decisions. A split creates another fully simulated band, not a placeholder.
- **Leaving goes somewhere real.** People who leave either join an existing band or set off alone as a group of one. A loner forages, survives or doesn't, and may meet and join a band later.
- **No placeholder population.** Neighbors' sizes and ages come from their own births and deaths. The 22-person top-up and the removal at 65 go away.

**Why it's worth doing before discovery:**
- Your founding question is how isolated cultures diverge. That needs several real groups developing in parallel, and ideas spreading or failing to spread between them.
- The calibration numbers (deaths, band sizes, how much tech helps) won't mean anything while people keep vanishing into placeholder bands.

**Costs:**
- It's a substantial refactor, since the sim is built around a single band.
- Runs get slower, roughly in proportion to the number of bands. Six bands would mean about 4 minutes per 80-year run instead of 40 seconds. I'd profile and trim afterward.

So far this session:
- **Fixed:** the crash and the leave-and-come-back loop. Bonds now fade while people live apart, and the choice to return weighs food prospects. Starvation fell from 63% to 44% of deaths.
- **Paused:** the land-richness and technology comparison, since those numbers depend on this refactor.

Shall I go ahead with the multi-band version?

### Claude (Assistant)

On the GPU: it won't help here, and I'd rather not burn time on it. GPUs are fast when thousands of identical, simple calculations run side by side. This sim is the opposite: a few hundred people, each making branching decisions (weighing options, remembering, choosing). That kind of work runs slower on a GPU.

What will help:
1. **More CPU cores.** Your laptop has many cores, and the test runs already use 6 at once. With multiple bands, each band can run on its own core, kept in step once a simulated day so results stay repeatable.
2. **Profiling.** The earlier speedups (camp moving, conversations) came from finding wasteful code. I'll do another pass once the multi-band version works.

Plan: build the multi-band version first, running bands one after another, and get it correct. Then profile, then run bands in parallel. Checking the machine and code layout:

### Claude (Assistant)

Short version: the multi-band world works, but the first long test shows populations exploding, and I know why.

**Done:**
- **Every band is real now.** Every band is fully simulated on a 40 km map. People move between bands only by walking: visits (to see kin or court), marrying into another band, joining a band they have ties to, or striking out alone as a new group.
- **The crash is fixed**, and there are 11 passing tests, including checks that everyone is in exactly one place and nobody leaves the map.
- **About 2× faster** per simulated year. On your hardware the bigger win is running 12 seeds at once.

**What the 12-seed, 80-year test showed:**

| | Sim | Real foragers |
|---|---|---|
| Population | 100 → 1,200–2,500 (45–95 bands) | roughly flat, 160–320 on this map |
| Births per 1,000 per year | 44 | ~40 ✓ |
| Deaths per 1,000 per year | 15 | ~35–40 |
| Main causes of death | starvation + cold 89%, illness ~2% | mostly illness, especially young children |

**Two causes:**
1. **The land is about 8× too rich.** I set its richness when there was a single band that only used the area around its camp. Now bands spread over the whole map, so all of it gets used. The old calibration only looked right because most of the land sat empty.
2. **Illness hardly kills anyone.** Bands split into many small groups that move often, which keeps disease low (that part is realistic). But childhood illness is too mild. In real forager groups about 20% of infants die and about 40–50% of children die before 15, mostly from infections.

**Next, unless you'd rather steer:**
1. Lower the land richness until the map settles at the real density of 160–320 people. Bands will then need to move camp more often, as real bands did.
2. Calibrate childhood illness against real infant and child death rates.
3. Rerun the technology comparison on that calibrated world.

Starting step 1 now as a sweep across land-richness values. I'm adding a test-only `--plants` flag so I can sweep without rebuilding.

### Claude (Assistant)

I've found the root of the food problem, and fixing it involves a design choice from you, so I'm stopping here.

**The food economy rests almost entirely on plants.**

| | Sim (current land richness) | Real temperate foragers |
|---|---|---|
| Food from hunting | 1–3% | roughly 40–60% of calories from animals, much of it fish |
| Firewood hours | more than foraging hours | about an hour or two a day per household |
| Fishing | doesn't exist, though the map has a river | a major food source, especially in spring |

Because meat barely counts, I inflated the plants (2.4) to keep bands alive. With bands now spread across the whole map, that inflated land feeds 2,000 people instead of about 250. Lower the plants and bands starve in the first spring, which is the real "hungry gap". Real foragers got through it on stored food, fish runs, and hunting.

**What I'd do, in order:**
1. **Find out why hunting barely happens.** Hunting is fully built (physical hunts, animal fear, risk weighed against reward), but people almost never choose it. My guess: hunts fail often, so people learn that hunting pays poorly per hour, and caution adds to that. Real hunters keep at it despite low success because meat is shared and hunting earns standing. I'll diagnose before changing anything.
2. **Bring firewood down to real levels.** Either the burn rate or the wood gathered per trip is off.
3. **Start the story in late summer** instead of spring with empty stores. That avoids a massacre caused purely by when the story begins.
4. **Recalibrate land richness downward** to the real density, then do childhood illness, then rerun the technology comparison.

**The decision for you:** should I add **fishing**? It isn't in the design yet. It would fit the existing system as a new skill area in the discovery web: hand-catching and spearing to start, then weirs and traps, nets, and hooks. Fish runs would be seasonal and tied to the river cells. Without it, hunting and gathering have to carry the whole calorie load, which is less true to how people lived near rivers.

Also, the crash at low land richness is fixed. A band that died out was still being checked for wolf attacks the same day.

I want to rework how the social layer works. Plan it with me first, don't build yet.

The problem: the social behaviors are designed events with organic inputs, not emergent behavior. There's a ConsiderTheft function, a ConsiderRaid function, AskForFood, Fight (Social.cs, plus whatever's changed since). The inputs are personal (hunger, warmth, trust, culture), but theft exists because we wrote it. Who gets targeted is our rule, and punishment is our formula. Nobody in the sim ever discovers that taking works or that thieves get punished.

What I want instead is "primitives + learning", so society emerges from people:

1. Small primitive acts instead of meaningful verbs: take food from a store, give, ask, refuse, attack, confront, shun/avoid, tell others (gossip), invite others to join you (generalize the hunt recruitment). There's no "steal" or "raid" verb. Theft is just taking from a store that isn't yours, and the chronicle labels it after the fact. The animation quiver stays about the same.

2. Real consequences, not scripted reactions. Nothing automatic happens after a take. What follows is whatever others choose to do with the same primitives. Only a thin "biology" layer is authored:
   - perception: who notices what, how far news spreads
   - a few innate feelings: anger at a loss you can pin on someone (stronger when your kids are hungry), fear when hurt, attachment to kin, maybe guilt or shame
   - the physics of food, fights, and injury
   - drives: food, safety, family, standing, comfort

3. People learn what actions lead to, in context. This is the ML part: a contextual bandit, or a tiny readable linear model per person. Context features are things like my hunger and my family's, whose store it is (kin, friend, distrusted), whether anyone's watching, and the owner's strength and temper. People learn from their own results over the following hours and days (food gained, refused, hit, shunned, standing lost), from watching others, and from gossip, which uses the existing belief spreading. The decider stays the same idea (score, add a little noise, pick), but scores for primitives come from these learned expectations.

Expected payoff: norms against theft (or a culture of taking), punishment, feuds, guarding stores, protection deals, maybe murder and revenge, all emerging rather than authored, and differing between worlds. It's also the machinery religion-by-misinterpretation needs anyway (for example, an omen right after a theft could make people believe the god punishes theft). Everything stays readable for the mind view and stays shapeable by god powers through named traits and beliefs.

Known costs, to plan around:
- someone has to try something first (exploration via curiosity or desperation), so some worlds will be quiet
- learning is slower and noisier
- societies can get stuck in degenerate states
- group actions like raids need coordination
- more compute
- the chronicle has to interpret primitives

Related idea for after this: evolved per-person values. Each person's drive weights are inherited with mutation, adjusted by experience, and copied culturally, so bands evolve their own decision styles instead of using global constants I picked.

What I want from you:
1. Check the current code first. Say where things stand: the Region and multi-band work, and anything that's changed in the social layer.
2. Give your honest take: does this fit the vision, and what would you change?
3. Propose a staged plan, starting with food and conflict (replacing AskForFood, ConsiderTheft, Fight, and ConsiderRaid with primitives, perception, innate feelings, and learned context-expectations), then extending to pairing and jealousy, leadership, and splitting.
4. Say where it should go in the build order relative to cooking and preservation, and the discovery system.

### Claude (Assistant)

Here's the social-layer plan. Food work continues right after; nothing here depends on it.

## 1. Where things stand

**Multi-band (built and working).** A `Region` owns the 40 km map, the clock, the animals, and every band. Each band is fully simulated. People move between bands only by walking: visits, pairing across bands, joining a band camped nearby, striking out alone, and splits that found new bands.

**Social layer: unchanged in kind, so your critique still applies in full.**
- **Within a band:** all designed events with organic inputs.
  - `AskForFood` and `ConsiderTheft` run from Eat when the household is short. Theft fires straight after a refusal, and the witnesses, the fight chance, and the trust penalties are all formulas.
  - `Fight` combines fight physics with a scripted reaction.
  - `ConsiderRaid` runs daily against hoarders, with built-in coordination and a fixed 70% seized.
  - `ShareMeat` and `BlameFailedHunt` handle hunt outcomes.
  - Strife, discontent, and splitting.
  - Norms (valor, sharing, peace) shift through authored lessons, such as "a raid worked, so peace drops".
- **Between bands:** what I added in the last session (pairing across bands, joining, Visit, striking out) is also designed decisions. There is **no conflict between bands at all** yet, so wars belong in this rework.

## 2. Honest take

It fits the vision well. It's the "nothing scheduled, nothing global" principle applied to behavior, and religion born of misread omens needs exactly this machinery: people linking acts to outcomes. Changes I'd make:

1. **Don't start everyone as a blank slate.** Founders should inherit a cultural prior: their band's existing beliefs about taking, sharing, and retaliation, varied per band. Humans had about 300,000 years of culture before this story begins. Blank learners would make year 0 an alien world and are the main route into stuck, degenerate societies. Learning then moves those priors.
2. **Use learned expectations sorted by context type rather than a free linear model, at first.** For example, "taking from a non-kin store while watched leads to…", plus a few continuous modifiers like hunger and the owner's strength. This plugs into the existing `Expectations` system, so it can be copied, gossiped, and taught for free, and the mind view can show it in a sentence. Learned weights can't be gossiped. A linear model can come later where it earns its keep.
3. **Credit assignment is the hard part. Make consequences carry their reason.** When someone confronts a thief, they do it *because of* a remembered act, so the reaction points back to that act. The thief then experiences "Ka hit me because Ka saw me take" and learns from it. That keeps learning causal without magic.
4. **Make trust a prediction.** Trust becomes "how I expect X to treat me", learned from what X has done. That replaces the hand-written trust penalties. Grievances (who wronged me, how badly, fading over time) are the innate anger, and they feed confront, attack, shun, and tell.
5. **One group primitive: "invite to a joint act."** Generalized from hunt recruitment: the inviter proposes, and each invitee weighs it with their own expectations plus their bond to the inviter. Hunts, raids, camp moves, splits, and wars all become this.
6. **Wars come from the same pieces.** Group identity is just a context feature ("target is from another band"). Two thin innate pieces need authoring:
   - **Grievance spreads from the individual to their group.** Revenge aimed at any member of the killer's band is well documented.
   - **Noticing outsiders in places you remember using.** This is a loss you can pin on them, which gives a notion of territory.

   Repeated retaliation becomes a feud, and a feud becomes war. Gifts between bands, plus learning that reciprocity pays, give alliances and trade partners. Who fights whom, and why, differs between worlds.

## 3. Staged plan

| Stage | What | Replaces |
|---|---|---|
| **0. Foundations** | Perception (who sees what, how news spreads); episode memory; grievances and innate feelings; the learner (own outcomes, watching others, gossip); the chronicle interpreting acts after the fact | nothing yet, built alongside |
| **1. Food and conflict within a band** | Primitives: take, give, ask, refuse, confront, attack, shun, tell. Fight physics stays. | `AskForFood`, `ConsiderTheft`, Fight-as-reaction, `ConsiderRaid`, the authored norm lessons |
| **2. Group acts** | Invite and join | hunt recruitment, camp-move votes, raids |
| **3. Between bands** | The same primitives across bands; group grievance; territory; raids, feuds, war; gifts and alliances | (new) |
| **4. Pairing and jealousy, leadership, splitting** | Mate guarding, affairs, couple separation (already approved). Leadership as learned "following X works out", which is what prestige becomes. Splitting as an invitation: "come with me". | courtship rules, prestige formula, `ConsiderSplitting` |
| **5. Evolved per-person values** | Drive weights inherited with mutation, shaped by experience, copied culturally | my global weights |

Each stage gets checked against ethnography. For example, in real bands most enforcement is gossip and shunning rather than fights, and theft within a band is rare. Each also gets checked across seeds, to confirm worlds really diverge rather than collapse into constant fighting.

## 4. Build order

1. **Food calibration**, now: hunting, firewood, a late-summer start, fishing, land richness, childhood illness. Conflict is driven by scarcity, so tuning learned behavior on a broken food economy would be wasted work.
2. **Social stages 0–3**, including wars. Contact and conflict between bands are central to your founding question about how cultures diverge.
3. **The discovery system**, reusing the stage 0 learner. Experimenting is "try something, see what happens", and teaching and spreading ideas between bands needs the contact rules from stage 3.
4. **Social stages 4–5**, then a full calibration pass, then the god, favor, and belief systems.

**Performance:** thanks for plugging in. Changing Windows power settings is off limits for me, but for the most speed you can pick "Best performance" under Settings → System → Power. I'll raise the parallel runs from 12 to about 20.

Once you've had a look, I can add the plan to `docs/PLANNING.md` as a proposal. Now continuing with the food work, starting with why nobody hunts.

- **Multi-band map:** working. The first 12-seed run showed populations exploding, because land richness was tuned back when one band only used the land around its camp.
- **Hunting:** went from 1–3% of food to 20–45%. Three causes:
  - **Too few animals.** There were 30–70× fewer than in reality. Species now use real field densities per km² (for example deer 4, boar 2).
  - **Bookkeeping bugs.** Hunts were judged against old wounds, and meat eaten at the kill went uncounted.
  - **Skewed memory.** Hunters believed hunting paid 0.03 food an hour when it really paid 0.28, because bad trips were remembered more strongly than good ones. People now remember what an activity yields as a fair long-run average.
- **Smaller fixes:** wooden spears reach 12 m, the story starts in late summer, and wolves only attack someone alone near a starving pack. Firewood turned out to be fine at about 1.8 hours per household per day.
- **Fishing:** added. Hand-catching is known at the start; rivers hold fish with spring and autumn runs; there's a Fish activity, a fishing skill, and methods from spear to weir and net.
- **Childhood illness:** chest infections in babies all year, dangerous weaning years, and protection from the mother's milk.
- **Fat reserves:** adults now carry 18–42 days' worth instead of 5–9. Of 153 starvation deaths, 142 came in spring and most were prime-age adults, because their reserves ran out within a week.

**Latest result over 40 years:**

| Land richness | Map population | Child mortality (infant / before 15) | Share of deaths from starvation |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1.0 | about 90–115 | 7–18% / 30–44% (close to real) | 84% |
| 1.4 | about 200–320 | 5% / 13–19% (too low) | 73% |

**Left to do:**
1. Find out why starvation still dominates after the reserve fix.
2. Settle land richness at about 1.2 and fine-tune childhood illness, so the map holds 160–320 people and illness is the main killer. Then re-run the tests and the technology comparison.
3. Social rework, stages 0–3 including wars between bands, per the plan we agreed. Then the discovery system.

### Claude (Assistant)

Short answers first, then a quick test on the spear question.

**Is food storage in?** Yes:
- **Keeping foods:** nuts and grain keep for months.
- **Preserved food:** drying, racks, smoking, and pemmican go into a separate store that keeps. Doing it requires the "keeping food" stage, which isn't known at the start.
- **Storage stages:** caches, pits, baskets, raised caches, and cold caches all slow spoilage.
- **Stockpiling:** people put food by in autumn based on how lean they remember winters being.

The catch is that every one of those methods is an unlock. The discovery system isn't built yet, so in normal runs nobody can learn them; they only appear when I hand them out with the test-only `--grant` flag. That's part of why spoilage is huge and spring is so deadly. Building discovery after the social rework fixes this.

**Spear fishing:** yes, it's the "spear fishing" stage. It needs wooden spears plus hand-catching, and it roughly doubles the catch over hand-catching. Like every unlock it can't be discovered yet, so right now everyone fishes by hand.

**Wooden spears:** you're right, and I blurred something your design separates. **Light throwing spears** (20 m range) are an unlock. When I raised the starting wooden spear from 8 m to 12 m, citing the ancient Schöningen spears, I effectively handed out part of that unlock for free. I think the right call is to put it back to 8 m and keep throwing as the discovery. Testing now whether hunting still holds up without it. I expect the real fixes were the animal densities and the learning bug.

### Claude (Assistant)

Spears are now a real thrusting length, and I've logged everything in `docs/PROGRESS.md`. The food side is in decent shape; what's left is starvation, and the next fix for that is the social rework.

**Spears.** Thrusting reach is now 2.5 m (a 2 m spear held partway along, plus a lunge). Since nobody can creep that close to a healthy deer, a party can instead drive a herd into marsh or river, where the animals flounder. That needs about six people, and slower animals are easier. Ambush at water and game trails is still to come with a later hunting pass.
- Hunting now supplies about 5–15% of food, which fits how hard thrusting-spear hunting really was.
- Handing everyone throwing spears cuts starvation's share of deaths from 47% to 32%. So that unlock has a clear, real payoff once discovery exists.

**What else is going on:**
- **Calibrated world:** infant and child mortality are close to real foragers. Illness and starvation are each roughly 40–45% of deaths, and the map holds about 250–350 people at 60 years.
- **Technology effects:** fishing helps most (about +30% population). Cooking helps a little. Preservation now works, but makes no clear difference yet.
- **Shared fires:** someone whose fire is out can warm up at a relative's or friend's hearth. Fires had been out about 40% of household-days.
- **Tests:** all 11 passed after the illness and fertility calibration; I haven't re-run them since the preservation, hearth, and spear changes.

**The real remaining problem: about 45% of deaths are still spring starvation.** It isn't the band running out of food. Particular households starve while others in the same camp still have food. Real foragers shared on demand, and that kind of sharing is what the social rework replaces: proper giving, asking, refusing, and the reputations that come from it. The other missing brake on population, violence between groups, also comes with that rework.

So unless you'd like something else first, I'll start the social rework now: stage 0 (who notices what, memory of who did what, grievances, and learning what acts lead to), then stage 1 (food and conflict within a band). I'll keep the existing tests passing as I go.

### Claude (Assistant)

Before writing code, here's the concrete design for stages 0 and 1. It replaces four designed events (`AskForFood`, `ConsiderTheft`, the scripted fight reactions, `ConsiderRaid`) plus the post-hunt sharing formula.

**Stage 0: foundations**
- **Deeds.** Every social act (ask, give, refuse, take, confront, attack) becomes a record of who did what to whom, and from whose hearth.
- **Perception.** Who notices a deed depends on who is awake in camp and nearby. Witnesses remember it, and people pass on memorable deeds when they talk, so news travels person to person.
- **Grievance (innate anger).** A loss you can pin on someone makes you angry at them, more so when your children are hungry. Anger fades with time.
- **Trust becomes a prediction.** Trust only moves when you see or hear what someone actually did: taking lowers it, giving raises it. No hand-written penalties.
- **Learning in context.** Each person learns what an act tends to lead to, given the situation: for example, taking from a non-relative's hearth while watched, versus unwatched. Results arrive later (getting confronted, beaten, refused), and the consequence carries its reason, so it gets credited to the act that caused it. People also learn by watching others and through gossip, which already spreads every belief.
- **Cultural starting point.** Founders inherit their band's existing expectations about taking, sharing, and retaliation, varied per band, instead of starting as blank slates.

**Stage 1: food and conflict in a band**
- **Asking and taking become ordinary choices.** A hungry person weighs them against foraging or fishing, scored by learned expected food plus innate feelings: guilt about harming kin or friends, and warmth.
- **Owners decide.** The household asked chooses to give or refuse based on warmth, the sharing norm, kinship, what it can spare, and learned reciprocity. Refusing has consequences only through what the refused person later chooses to do.
- **Nothing is automatic.** Victims and witnesses may confront or attack; the thief chooses whether to hand the food back. Fights keep their physics.
- **Hunters share by choice.** Hunters' households decide what to give to whom using the same giving decision.
- **Raids come back in stage 2**, built from "invite others to join".
- **The chronicle labels events after the fact:** "X took food from Y's hearth" becomes a theft.

Writing this into `PLANNING.md`, then building.

### Claude (Assistant)

### Claude (Assistant)

All five social stages and the discovery system are built. All 11 tests pass, and every change is logged in `docs/PROGRESS.md`.

**What's in now:**
- **Stages 0–1, food and conflict:**
  - Asking, giving, refusing, taking, confronting, and attacking are now ordinary choices. Nothing follows automatically: consequences are whatever other people choose to do.
  - People learn what each act tends to lead to (taking from a stranger while watched, versus unwatched, for example) from their own results, from watching others, and from gossip.
  - Anger and guilt are innate, and trust is learned from what people actually do.
  - The old scripted theft, raid, and sharing code is gone.
- **Discovery:**
  - All 97 discoverable stages can be stumbled on during ordinary work, when someone has the know-how it builds on, the materials at hand, and the right pressure (hunger, cold, food rotting, and so on).
  - Know-how passes through conversation, children watching adults work, and visits between bands, and it's lost when the last person who knows it dies.
  - Each world finds about 17–28 stages in 60 years.
- **Stage 2, group force:** anyone can rally others to take a hearth's food by force. People choose whether to join or defend, and the fight is physical.
- **Stage 3, between bands:**
  - A harm from one person turns into anger at their whole band.
  - People from different bands meet on shared foraging ground, usually peacefully. Ideas and illness pass between them, and sometimes fights break out.
  - Raids at first light, with fear of the enemy, revenge cooling anger, and bands moving away from enemies holding feuds in check.
  - Violence ends up at 7–13% of deaths, in the realistic range.
- **Stage 4:**
  - Jealousy, and couples who sometimes separate.
  - Leadership as learned trust: people judge whether following someone's camp moves and hunts paid off.
- **Stage 5:** every person has inherited values for food, safety, family, standing, comfort, and company. Experience shifts them for a while, and childhood shapes them for life, so bands grow their own ways of deciding.

**The biggest find:** a bug where eating was offered whenever the *camp* had food, but only drew from one's *own* hearth. People with an empty hearth kept choosing to eat and starved without ever asking or foraging. That was the hidden cause of the "spring starvation" we had been chasing for a long while. With it fixed, illness is the main killer, as it was for real foragers.

**Worlds now differ in ways I didn't script.** Some are peaceful and others feud. One band in an earlier run learned that taking even when seen pays: a culture of taking. Breakups range from none to dozens, and bands differ in how much they share and value company.

**Your decision:**
- **Discovery pacing.** Right now about 15–20 stages get found per generation across the whole map, far fewer per band. That's slow enough that your god's nudges would clearly matter, but it's your call how fast natural discovery should feel.

**Still open:**
- Final land-richness calibration (populations are a little above the real target).
- Speed: about 4 seconds per simulated year.
- Ambush hunting.
- Turning the last few hand-written lessons ("hard times teach sharing") into learned ones.

### Claude (Assistant)

I wrote the full review to [docs/REVIEW.md](docs/REVIEW.md). Here's the core of it.

**The vision:** how would isolated cultures develop without colonization? You play a new god who can't act directly and is often misunderstood; religion grows out of that misreading. Progress comes when conditions in a discovery web converge. People are inspectable minds, biology is grounded, worlds are invented, and the arc runs toward modern-ish. The target is a real game by about summer 2027.

**Where we are:**
- **Done and calibrated:**
  - Real-scale world and ecology.
  - Bodies, disease, and population numbers that match forager data.
  - A food economy with foraging, hunting, fishing, cooking, and storage.
  - Every band fully simulated.
  - All five social stages.
  - A working discovery engine.

  Worlds already diverge without scripting (peaceful against feuding, sharing against taking cultures), which partly answers your founding question.
- **Partial:**
  - Only **30 of 97** discoveries actually change anything. Language, cordage, baskets, traps, dogs, pottery, grain, burial, and ochre do nothing yet.
  - Evolution is inheritance only.
- **Not started:** the god layer (favor, prayer, omens, powers, prophet, blessed births), religion, language effects, domestication, agriculture, trade, islands, alien fauna, codex, Unity.

**The honest part:** the simulation underneath is deep, but **the game itself hasn't been started**. The first milestone called for 4–5 god powers and belief forming from your actions, and neither exists. About 8 months remain, and art is the likely bottleneck.

**Priorities, in order:**
1. **P0:**
   - The god layer.
   - Religion by misinterpretation, built on the association learning that already exists.
   - Language with real effects.
2. **P1:** give effects to the other 67 discoveries, deepen discovery (alternative routes, a deliberate "tinker" activity, real accidents), and a codex.
3. **P2:** a simple Unity slice early, speed for real time, save/load.
4. **P3:** domestication and farming, trade and hierarchy, islands and first contact, evolution pathways, final calibration.

**Hard-code audit, worst first:**
- **A (high).** Code still tells bands what to learn: hard times raise sharing, big kills raise valor. The sharing/valor/peace norms now overlap with the learned expectations about giving, taking, and attacking, and should be retired.
- **B (high).** Joining a raid, escalating a fight, pairing, and separating are decided by comparing a score to a fixed bar. They should compare against the person's best alternative, as joining a hunt already does.
- **C.** Separation happens on a daily dice roll instead of being a choice when an occasion arises; raided camps wake at a fixed 60%.
- **D/E.**
  - Takes and raids grab fixed fractions of a store instead of what a person can physically carry.
  - Prestige is a formula (meat ÷ 100) instead of how others regard someone.
- **F.** About 34 checks use fixed ages instead of body maturity and a culture's idea of "grown up".
- **H.**
  - Everyone knows where every band within 15 km is camped.
  - Raids resolve instantly.
  - "Children stay with the mother" is an authored rule.
- **Fine as authored:** world facts, my fitted calibration settings, the innate feelings, and perception.

**My recommendation:** spend a day or two on audit items A and B (cheap, and they close the biggest gaps with your principle), then move to the god layer.

Do you agree with switching to the god layer next, and is a day or two on A and B first the right call?

### Claude (Assistant)

Here's the full picture, with evidence from the six 60-year worlds I last ran. Some of it is uncomfortable.

## 1. How learning works, and what it did

**How it works:** each person holds expected payoffs for acts in context: asking a relative, taking from a stranger's hearth while watched, attacking an outsider, following a particular leader. Values move from:
- **their own results**, such as food gained, or being refused or beaten;
- **later consequences**, which carry the act that caused them (a confrontation over a take counts against that take);
- **watching others** get punished, at a lower weight;
- **gossip:** conversations pass a few beliefs and recent deeds each time.

Children start from their mother's expectations, and founders from their band's culture.

**What I saw:**
- **It works in the intended direction.** Founders start expecting a seen take to cost about −1 ration. By year 60, bands sit at −4 to −14, and one band in an earlier run reached **+4**: a culture where taking pays.
- **Red flag 1: a bug in crediting consequences.** When a consequence arrives, I add it on top of the current expectation instead of re-averaging the deed's total payoff. Repeated consequences ratchet values without limit, which is why −14 is possible. The fix is to keep each deed's running total and learn toward that.
- **Red flag 2: theft waves without punishment.** Seed 4's founding band went from about 40 takes a year to **641 and 763 a year** around years 29–35, with **zero fights** in those years. Anger exists, but confronting someone competes with foraging as just another option. Angry people rarely choose it, so takes go unanswered and takers learn that taking pays. Real bands answer theft mainly through gossip, shunning, and refusing the thief. Refusals do happen here, but turning away a known thief isn't a strong enough or deliberate enough response yet.

## 2. Discoveries in the runs

- **15–28 stages known per world** by year 60, and they spread widely. The founding band itself worked out only **0–3**, but learned **15–27** from other bands. Diffusion through visits and meetings dominates, much like real prehistory.
- **Found in most worlds:** caches, drying, hide wraps, storytelling, torches, burning the land, cordage, baskets.
- **Found in only some worlds:** throwing spears and spear-throwers in 3 of 6, bows in 2.
- **Impact:** only **30 of 97** stages change anything. In controlled tests where I handed technology out, fishing tech raised population about **30%** and throwing spears cut starvation from **47% to 32%**. In these natural runs I **didn't measure** before/after effects, so I can't claim specific discoveries changed a world's course. A per-band before/after report should be added.

## 3. Good signs and red flags

**Good:**
- Child mortality and causes of death are realistic.
- Violence makes up 7–13% of deaths.
- Worlds diverge on peace, sharing, taking, and values.
- Ideas spread between bands.
- No crashes.

**Red flags:**
- The learning bug above, and the theft waves.
- **Sharing is stuck at 0.31–0.34 in most bands.** That's evidence for audit item A: code pulls sharing toward 0.3 whenever times are good.
- Couple breakups vary from 0 to 125 per band, likely because of the fixed threshold (B) and the daily dice roll (C).
- Rallies almost never happen.
- One world had cold causing 35% of deaths (seed 2), not yet explained.
- Populations sit a little above target, and speed is about 4 seconds per simulated year.

## 4. The audit, with evidence

| | Problem | Evidence | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A** | Code tells bands what to learn: hard times raise sharing, good times pull it toward 0.3, kills raise valor (`Simulation.cs:323, 324, 615, 638, 974`) | Sharing ends up at 0.31–0.34 in most bands: the authored pull, not experience | Retire the sharing/valor/peace norms. They already overlap with learned give/attack/take expectations, which should drive these choices |
| **B** | Choices compared to fixed bars: join a raid if > 0.6, escalate if > 0.4, pair if > 0.35, separate if the gap > 0.3 | Breakups 0–125, rallies near zero: outcomes hinge on where a bar happens to sit | Compare against the person's best alternative, as joining a hunt already does |
| **C** | Behavior on dice timers: separating is a 0.5%/day roll; a raided camp wakes at a fixed 60% | The same | Decide when an occasion arises (a fight, a new attachment, a lean season); waking depends on dogs, watches, and time of day |
| **D** | Fixed fractions: a take grabs up to 30% of a store, a raid 60% | Every take is roughly the same size | Physical carrying capacity, about 10–15 rations a person, more with baskets |
| **E** | Prestige = meat ÷ 100 (`Hunt.cs:419`) | Standing is one global number | Standing is how each person regards someone, learned like trust (the leader-following data already exists) |
| **F** | About 34 fixed ages (14 or 16 for adult choices) | Code count | Body maturity, plus a cultural "grown up" that can differ by band |
| **G** | Hand-set weights inside formulas | Code | Partly mitigated by personal drives; keep shrinking them |
| **H** | **Omniscience:** people know the exact food in every patch, every herd within 10 km, every band within 15 km | See section 5 | **Mental maps** (your idea) |

## 5. How people find things now, and mental maps

**Right now it's mostly omniscient:**
- **Foraging:** each day the band gets the 25 best plant patches within 4 km, ranked by *true* current stock, and foragers pick among the top 8. Curious people sometimes try random spots up to 7 km out. Per-season memory of yields exists, but it's only used when proposing camp moves.
- **Firewood and fishing:** the best spots by true amounts.
- **Hunting:** the party knows the exact position of **every herd within 10 km**. "Tracking" is just a 20–70% chance per hour based on skill.
- **Other bands:** everyone knows where every band within 15 km is camped.

**Your mental-map idea fixes all of these, and it fits the game:**
- **Each person carries a map of what they've seen or been told.** It covers plant patches by season, wood, flint, fishing spots, game sightings and trails, and other bands' camps. Entries fade with time and stay vague.
- **Animals leave fresh signs** in the cells they pass. Hunters find herds by finding signs near where they expect game, and skill decides how well they read them.
- **Places spread through talk**, and elders know the most. Exploring becomes a real activity, done more by the curious.
- **Map-making as tiered discoveries** improving on that:
  1. **Mental maps** at the start.
  2. **Named places and routes**, tied to storytelling and plant lore, so places spread much better in talk.
  3. **Ground sketches**, so people can show others distant places, plan moves and raids, and coordinate hunts.
  4. **Carried maps** on hide or bark.
  5. **Landmark and star navigation**, which later feeds sailing.
- **It makes god powers meaningful.** A herd you place only matters once someone finds it. Omens could point people toward it, which is your "influence, not control" pillar.

## 6. Hunting now, and tactics

**Now:**
1. Gather a party.
2. Each member names the herd they'd go after, by their own weighing of gain, glory, fear, and distance; the most influential choice wins.
3. Walk out and track.
4. Get close: either creep within 2.5 m, or drive the herd into marsh or river if the party is big enough.
5. Strike, with hits adding up against the animal's size.
6. Wounded animals may charge.
7. Chase, butcher, carry home, share.

There's one fixed approach per terrain and nothing else.

**Proposed tactics,** chosen by the party from learned expectations for each tactic, animal, and terrain, so hunting styles become culture:

| Tactic | What it needs | Known at start or discovered |
|---|---|---|
| Stalk (creep up) | Patience, stealth | Known |
| Ambush at water or game trails | Knowing the trails (mental map), waiting for hours | Known |
| Drive into bog, water, or dead ends | 5 or more people, terrain | Known |
| Surround / encircle | A large party, coordination | Discovered |
| Persistence chase | Heat, endurance | Discovered |
| Fire drive | Burning the land | Discovered |
| Pit traps, game fences | Digging, settled camps | Discovered |
| Dogs | Dog stages | Discovered |
| Snares and traps | A separate everyday activity using the existing trap stages | Discovered |

Roles within a party would come from traits: the bold strike, the fast drive, the patient wait.

## Suggested order

1. **Fix the learning bug and the response to theft** (about a day). Both corrupt everything built on top.
2. **Audit items A and B** (a day or two).
3. **Mental maps and finding things,** including signs, scouting, place gossip, and the map-making tiers.
4. **Hunting tactics** (they need the mental maps for trails).
5. **Then the god layer.** Mental maps come first because placing animals and resources, and omens, depend on people having to find things.

Shall I go in that order? And should map-making become its own tile in the discovery web (the five tiers above), or be folded into the existing language and plant-lore stages?

### Claude (Assistant)

Steps 1–4 are done, plus most of step 5: the learning bugs, audit items A–F, mental maps with map-making, and hunting tactics. **82 of 103 discoveries** now change something. All 11 tests pass. Populations still grow about 1.5% a year, which I've left as the main open item. The full log is at the end of `docs/PROGRESS.md`.

**Learning and theft**
- A single theft was being punished over and over, because every refusal counted against it again. Each consequence now counts once.
- Witnesses now learn the whole story (what the taker gained and what it cost them) instead of only the costs.
- A family whose food goes missing unseen suspects someone, usually a known taker, so unseen theft finally has consequences.
- Theft gets answered on the spot by whoever saw it.
- Taking only appeals to people who are actually going hungry.

**Audit**
- **A:** the hand-taught norms (sharing, valor, peace) are gone. Learned payoffs and personal drives do their job.
- **One shared scale for act choices.** Learned costs of violence used to be multipliers, so they could never make it look bad. They're now added on the same scale as food work, so a band that has learned hitting costs avoids it.
- **B:** joining a raid or rally now compares against what else the person could do. Defending, escalating, and meeting strangers compare against the alternative.
- **C:** couples consider separating on real occasions. Pair bonds are deep, and resentment keeps people from going straight back to an ex.
- **D:** what a person can carry (about 12 rations, more with baskets) now limits takes, raids, foraging, and kills brought home.
- **E:** standing is how each person regards someone, learned from experience, instead of one global prestige score.
- **F:** "grown up" is each band's own custom.
- **Two more brakes on violence:** people fear a target's kin and friends, and feuds push people to leave rather than fight.

**Mental maps and hunting**
- **Knowing the land:**
  - Founders know their home range.
  - People see what's around them and pass places on in talk.
  - The camp hears each evening what's left where.
  - A band you visit or raid can have moved on by the time you arrive.
- **Map-making is a new tile:** named places, ground maps, carried maps, star paths.
- **Hunting from real sightings:** hunts go after herds someone actually saw, track them from there, and pick a tactic. Stalking, ambush at water, and driving into wet ground are known at the start; surrounding and running prey down are discovered; fire drives use burning the land. Each band develops its own hunting habits.
- **Imperfect knowledge costs about 20%** of foraging returns, which is realistic.

**Bugs this turned up**
- **Hunting parties searching forever.** A party that set out to look for game kept looking even after finding a herd, and its members starved mid-hunt.
- **People attacking themselves** in group clashes.
- **Founders expecting hunting to pay 2.4× its real return**, so bands starved chasing prey they now had to find.

**Discoveries**
- **82 of 103 have direct effects now**, including:
  - trapping as a new everyday activity;
  - shelters and clothing for warmth;
  - plant lore and grinding for better foraging;
  - harpoons and canoes for fishing;
  - darts and better bows;
  - dogs and torches against wolves;
  - shared rites easing discontent;
  - a real farming path, where grain near long-used camps grows richer.
- **19 are stepping stones** for other stages. The art stages (ochre, figurines) wait for the religion layer.

**Calibration (6 worlds, 30–40 years)**
- Starvation and violence are both a small share of deaths, illness is the main killer, and about 46% of children die before 15, as among real foragers.
- Map populations are 175–288 at 40 years, inside the real target.
- Worlds still differ: some are peaceful, some feud, and hunting styles diverge.

**Speed:** conversations got 5× faster after I found a hidden squared cost in how game sightings were passed on. A run is about 40% faster overall.

**Flagged, not fixed tonight** (all logged with reasons):
- **Population still grows about 1.5% a year.** Real foragers held near zero. That needs a proper demography pass with life tables (birth spacing, age at first birth) rather than more setting-twiddling.
- **Breakups are rarer than among real foragers.** They need more causes, such as a partner who doesn't provide, childlessness, or conflict.
- **Violence takes a high *share* of deaths in a few worlds**, mostly because total deaths are low.
- **Wolf attacks still run on a daily dice roll.**
- **Discovery pacing** is still your decision.

Next up is the god layer, unless you'd rather I do the demography pass first.

- how far apart babies are spaced;
- how many children a woman has over her life;

- **Speed**, an ongoing job.

2. **Key Technical Concepts**

   **Structure and running**
   - Region (owns the world, clock, ecology and every band) versus Simulation (one band). Bands step hourly.
   - Deterministic RNG forks per band.
   - Parallel experiment runs (`compare.sh`, `metrics.py`, per band-year metrics compared against targets).

   **Decisions and learning**
   - Utility decisions: option scores plus noise, then argmax. `Decider.Options` and `BestAlternative`.
   - Learned expectations are string topics, e.g. `act:<act>:<relation>[:seen|unseen]`, `tactic:<t>:<species>`, `follow:<id>`, `lean:season`, `danger:x`.
   - Even-handed EMA for activity yields.
   - The `Felt` scale (rations × 0.6) converts payoffs to option scores.
   - Deeds (ask, give, refuse, take, confront, attack, return) with Payoff, AnsweredBy, Answers and Context; perception through Witness; innate Grievance, Guilt and fear; BandGrievance; gossip.

   **Society and culture**
   - Drives (Food, Safety, Family, Standing, Comfort, Company) with BaseDrives (settled character). Children inherit their parents' base drives and adopt teachers' bases.
   - Joint acts (Rally, DoWarRaid), encounters on shared ground, Visit, Pair across bands, ConsiderJoining, splits, strike-outs.
   - Band customs: ResidenceNorm, ChildrenFollowMother, ComingOfAge.

   **Knowledge of the land**
   - Mental maps (`PlaceNote`, sightings dictionary, known camps), LookAround, KnowHomeRange, TellPlaces, CampNews.

   **Hunting**
   - Hunts start from sightings, then tracking by drift and skill.
   - Tactics: Stalk, Ambush, Drive, Surround, Persistence, FireDrive.

   **Discovery**
   - The engine rolls first against a best case (machine-readable `sim` blocks: during, materials, pressures, interest, skill, trait, way, rate).
   - PassOn through talk, watching and visits.

   **Body and food**
   - Body reserves (MaxReserve, Nourishment).
   - Fertility: satiety³ × age curve × nursing suppression over 4 years.
   - Disease: care multiplies the body's own fight; case-fatality calibration; accidents.
   - Fishing with runs; trapping; carry capacity; grain tending (WildCapacity).

4. **Errors and fixes**

   **Crashes and bookkeeping**
   - **Duplicate people:** fixed by skipping people who left mid-hour.
   - **WolvesNearCamp on an empty band:** guards added.
   - **Bash heredoc quoting failures:** moved patches to Python files in the scratchpad.
   - **Expectations.Set recursion:** a regex replace rewrote Set's own body into a call to itself; fixed.
   - **`b` variable name clash:** fixed.

   **Behavior bugs**
   - **The Eat bug:** people with an empty hearth kept choosing to eat and starved.
   - **Self-attack in rallies:** "Kanor attacked Kanor"; fixed.
   - **Searching hunts never ended:** members starved mid-hunt; fixed.
   - **Founders' hunting prior 0.6:** caused a starvation spiral; lowered to 0.25.
   - **Random tactic habits:** bands chose hopeless stalking; replaced with physics-informed priors.

   **Learning and social dynamics**
   - **Refusals re-crediting the same take:** fixed with AnsweredBy.
   - **Witnesses learning only the immediate gain:** caused a theft explosion; switched to Payoff-based watcher learning.
   - **Removing norms collapsed giving:** fixed with Expects(Give) at full weight, kin altruism, refusal costs and full reciprocity.
   - **Multiplicative expectations:** couldn't deter violence; switched to the additive Felt scale.
   - **Wide violence priors:** gave violent bands from day one; tightened.
   - **Winning-a-fight +1 reward:** removed.
   - **Breakup flip-flops** (one person left 160 times): fixed with resentment in Desire, bond damage on parting, and stronger pair bonds.
   - **Drives ratcheting** (to 0 or 0.99): fixed with BaseDrives and relaxation.
   - **Distrusted taboo weight too low:** caused feud spirals; fixed.

   **Calibration**
   - **Animal densities 30–70× too low:** fixed.
   - **Learning bias on yields:** fixed with an even-handed EMA.
   - **Reserves only 5–9 days:** fixed.
   - **Care additive:** cared-for children beat infections on day one; now multiplicative.
   - **Spear range:** per user feedback, 12 m → 8 m → 2.5 m with drives and tactics.
   - **Storage:** per user, no starting storage.

   **Performance**
   - **TellPlaces:** O(n·m) over game sightings; fixed with a dictionary.

5. **Problem Solving**

   **Final calibrated state (6 worlds, 30–40 years):**
   - population 175–288;
   - starvation 0–8%;
   - illness the top cause of death;
   - killings at most 0.07 per band-year;
   - under-15 deaths ~46%;
   - growth ~1.5% per year.

   **Flagged in PROGRESS.md as open:**
   - population growth;
   - low breakups;
   - violence share high where total deaths are low;
   - wolf dice;
   - remaining global weights;
   - belief-layer stages (ochre hand/paint/figure, pot.pit, fire.lamp);
   - speed (~3.5 s per simulated year at ~200 people);
   - hunting ~0.09 rations per hour with thrusting spears.

   Discovery effects: 82 of 103 stages act directly; 19 are stepping stones.

The runs are still going: 6 worlds, about 20 seconds per simulated year each. I'll finish the audit when they're done. Here's what I've found so far.

**What the land can feed:** Running every cell's regrowth across the map gives these maximums, before anyone walks or carries anything:

| Season | Plants (rations/day) |
|---|---|
| Spring | ~1,500 |
| Summer | ~5,600 |
| Autumn | ~9,000 |
| Winter | ~850 |

On top of that, game adds about 150 rations a day and resident fish about 11. An average person eats about 0.75 rations a day. Winter is the bottleneck, so the land's theoretical ceiling is about 1,300 people. Since people can only reach part of the land, the realistic ceiling is roughly 400–650.

Real temperate foragers lived at about 0.05–0.3 people per km². On this 1,600 km² map that's 80–480 people. So the land's ceiling sits at the high end of real, but not crazy.

That supports your point. Starting small and growing is fine; what matters is whether growth levels off at that ceiling. Earlier 60-year runs reached 290–600 and were still climbing. The 120-year runs will show whether hunger ever starts holding the population in check.

The 6 test worlds are 72–88 years into the 120-year runs. The full life tables only print when they finish, but the yearly population totals already show the main problem.

**Population over time:**

| World | Y10 | Y30 | Y50 | Y70 | Y80 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 123 | 186 | 295 | 506 | — |
| 2 | 113 | 197 | 328 | 604 | — |
| 3 | 121 | 155 | 262 | 378 | 445 |
| 4 | 139 | 231 | 359 | 598 | — |
| 5 | 91 | 150 | 268 | 431 | 565 |
| 6 | 117 | 135 | 208 | 354 | 473 |

Growth doesn't level off. It speeds up instead: about 1.5% a year early on, 2.5–3.5% a year after year 50. Every world has already passed the 400–650 ceiling I estimated for the land, and passed 480, the top of what real foragers would number on a map this size.

So you're right that the problem isn't growth itself, it's the missing ceiling. Nothing slows growth as the land fills up. Three likely reasons, which I'll confirm or rule out when the tables finish:

1. **New bands claim fresh land for free.** Bands split and set up camp in empty country, so each band stays small and well fed in its own patch. The map-wide total can climb until the whole map is full, and the map can feed more than I estimated because nobody is competing yet.
2. **Hunger doesn't kick in early enough.** Women's fertility only drops when they get thin (fertility is scaled by how well fed they are, cubed). So births stay at full rate until people are actually starving. Real foragers felt crowding much earlier: longer walks to food, more work, slightly lower fertility and more disease well before outright hunger.
3. **Winter may be too easy.** Body fat carries people through several lean weeks, and hunting fills the gap, so the lean season never thins the population much.

**Still to come** when the runs finish (probably another 30–60 minutes, since bigger worlds run slower):
- death rates by age, and how long people live;
- births per woman, spacing between babies, and age at first birth;
- causes of death by age;
- how many people are paired, and how often couples break up.

Those will show whether the excess is too many births or too few adult deaths. That split decides the fix, and I'll bring both to you before changing anything.

I've also gone through the code for the other flags. Breakups are still too rare because the only reason anyone leaves is falling for someone free. Discovery pacing is your call. The wolf attack check is the 25%-a-day dice roll. I'll go through all of them alongside the demography results.

### Claude (Assistant)

### Claude (Assistant)

You're right about reason 1. Bands splitting off into empty land is how humans actually spread, so the sim doing it is correct. It also changes what I should be testing. Splitting doesn't need to be stopped. The test is what happens once the empty land runs out.

**What I meant by "no competition yet":** while bands are few and far apart, their foraging areas don't overlap. Each band is picking its own untouched patch, so there's nothing to compete over. That's real too: a growing population on an empty continent isn't held back by anything.

**How competition should work.** It should come from two structural sources, with no "territory" rule written in:

1. **Shared food running out.** This is mostly there already. Plants, fish and game are shared: if a neighbouring band strips a hillside of berries, there are fewer for you, and the herds are the same herds. Once foraging areas start to overlap, people should automatically face longer walks, emptier patches, thinner bodies, fewer conceptions and more deaths from disease. Growth should then level off by itself.
2. **Run-ins with other bands.** Meeting other people on shared ground already exists, as encounters, grudges and raids, and people already treat ground near enemies as risky. Territory can grow out of that: if a band keeps having bad meetings in some valley, its people learn to stay out of it. That's how real forager territories formed, as learned and defended habits rather than drawn borders.

**Why it might not be working.** The populations are already past where the land should start pushing back, and growth is speeding up rather than slowing. So something in source 1 isn't working. Things I'll check before claiming anything:
- **Off-map game.** The ecology keeps a region-wide pool for each animal. If it keeps restocking the map with herds from off the edge, there's free food that never runs out. If so, that's a hidden source of food from outside the map, and it breaks the closed-world rule.
- **The ceiling is just higher than my estimate.** My 400–650 was a rough calculation and could be well off. If so, the map should still fill and level off later, and the real question becomes whether that ceiling matches real forager density.
- **How far apart bands camp.** If new camps always find gaps between existing ones, foraging areas never overlap enough to bite until the very end.

When the runs finish, I'll look at how much food each person gets and how fed people are year by year. If people stay full while the population climbs past 600, food isn't the brake, and the next step is to find where the extra food is coming from. I'll tell you what I find before changing anything.

Found something on the off-map game question. Each animal species has a bigger region around the map, the "valley", and the herds in it:
- breed every year;
- send animals onto the map;
- receive migrating herds back.

Animals on the map do count against the region's total, so hunting does eventually thin the whole population. But the part off the map is never hunted, and it keeps restocking the valley every spring. So the map gets fed with game from a larger area than the 1,600 km² people actually live on.

That's partly real: migratory herds do come in from unhunted country. But it does mean the land can feed more people than the map alone could. I'll quantify how much once the runs finish, then fold it into the audit.

### Claude (Assistant)

We didn't lose everything, but we did lose the most detailed part.

**What happened.** Background jobs I start get killed after 2 hours; that's a hard limit on my tools. My analysis program only printed its tables at the very end, so when the runs were stopped those were lost. That part is my fault. What survived is the year-by-year population of all 6 worlds, which is the main finding: none of them levelled off, even at 800–1,150 people. What we lost is the detail: death rates by age, births per woman, birth spacing and pairing. The new version writes results as it goes, so a kill can't wipe it out again.

**Is 60 years enough?** It depends on which question:
- **For the life tables, yes.** Death and birth rates by age don't need a big population, just enough person-years. The current runs are already at years 41–52, so those tables will be ready in a few minutes.
- **For the carrying-capacity question, no.** You're right. We need to see what happens once the map fills up, which takes 120+ years and 1,000+ people. Two fixes:
  1. Launch those runs as separate processes on your laptop, outside my tool's 2-hour limit, writing results as they go so nothing is lost. 2–3 worlds instead of 6, so they don't fight over the machine.
  2. Or start a world already near full. That answers "does it level off?" without spending 100 simulated years filling the map first.

**Why it's slow.**
- **Big populations cost more than their size suggests.** The current runs managed about 50 years in roughly 15 minutes while small. At 200 people it's about 3.5 s per simulated year. At 1,000 people it's well over 20 s, because each person makes a decision every simulated hour, which is 8,760 decisions a year. Conversation and gossip grow with band size. Meetings between bands grow with the number of bands squared.
- **6 runs at once share one machine.** Your laptop has 24 cores, but heat and memory bandwidth slow each run down when they all go flat out. That's the fan noise.

Speeding up large populations is already on the flagged list, and it now matters more. If the map really should hold several hundred people, every long run pays this cost.

My plan: let these 60-year runs finish for the life tables, then launch 2–3 detached 150-year runs that write results as they go, so the levelling-off question gets a real answer.

### Claude (Assistant)

The audit is done. First, your hardware question.

**No, I wasn't using the laptop properly.** Each world runs on a single thread, so 6 worlds used at most 6 of your 24 threads, about 25% of the CPU. When you took the screenshot, 5 had already finished, which is why it showed one process at 4%. The fans were mostly heat from those few busy cores, not real load. Two ways to use it properly:
- **Run more worlds at once.** Easy, and helps immediately for testing.
- **Run a world's bands in parallel.** A real engineering job, because bands share land and animals and results have to stay repeatable.

Your laptop has Intel integrated graphics, and a GPU wouldn't help this kind of branchy, per-person logic anyway.

## Demography audit (6 worlds, 60 years, rates from years 30–60)

| Measure | Sim | Real foragers (Hadza / !Kung / Ache) | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| Births per woman | 5.9–7.5 | 4.5–6.2 | high |
| Age at first birth | 18.7–19.5 | ~19 | ✅ |
| Years between births (previous child alive) | 3.6–3.9 | 3.5–4 | ✅ |
| Infant deaths | 10–28% | 15–25% | ✅/low in 2 worlds |
| Reaching 15 | 62–80% | 55–65% | too many survive |
| Further years expected at 15 | 34–46 | ~37–40 | ✅ roughly |
| Average fed level, every year | **0.99** | lean seasons bite | ❌ |
| Food brought in vs needed | **1.25–1.6×** | ~1× | ❌ |
| Falls and drowning, share of deaths | **~30%** | 5–10% | ❌ |
| Breakups per world over 60 years | 14–34, almost all "left for someone else" | far more | low |
| Violence share of deaths | 19% in one world, ~0–1% in the others | 0–30%, varies | fine |
| Deaths from wolves | 0 | rare | fine |
| People per km² at year 60 | 0.17–0.29, still growing ~2.5%/yr | 0.05–0.3, flat | ❌ no ceiling |

## What it means

**1. Food never runs short. This is the root problem.** People average 0.99 fed in every year of every world, and bands bring in 25–60% more food than they need. Hunting alone covers about 100% of everyone's needs once bands learn it (by years 20–40). That's far too much: earlier, hunting gave only about 0.09 rations an hour. Two suspects:
- the off-map herds restocking the map every year;
- hunting tactics and weapons having become very effective.

Because nobody is ever thin, two other problems follow without needing their own fix:
- **Births stay at maximum.** Fertility scales with how well fed a woman is, cubed, and at 0.99 fed that factor never drops.
- **Too many children survive.** Well-fed children beat infections.

So high births and high child survival are symptoms. Once food runs short when bands crowd, both should come down, along with the population, without any extra fix. Tuning them directly would hide the real problem.

**2. Falls kill about 30% of everyone, which is a clear bug.** About 50 per world in 30 years, including children aged 5–14. The accident rate was set when people spent fewer hours out on the land. Long searching hunts and more trips out multiplied the exposure. Also, adults almost never die of illness, which is the main adult killer among real foragers.

**3. Breakups are too rare.** Almost all of them are someone leaving for a new partner. Couples nearly never just part. Same diagnosis as before: there need to be more reasons to leave, such as a partner who doesn't provide, no children, or a long-running feud.

**4. Violence: you were right.** It's one violent world and five peaceful ones, which looks like real variation, not a problem.

**5. Wolves are irrelevant to the numbers.** They caused zero deaths. The daily dice roll for attacks still deserves to become a real decision by the pack, but it isn't urgent.

**6. Discovery pacing is your call.** Currently about 32–42 different discoveries per world in 60 years. Do you want that faster, slower, or as is? Setting it slow leaves the god layer room to matter.

## Proposed fix order (no code changed yet)

1. **Work out where the extra food comes from.** Measure the off-map herd restocking and hunting returns per hour by tactic and weapon, then fix whatever isn't grounded in real data. Then run long enough to watch the map fill and confirm growth levels off near real densities.
2. **Accidents.** Tie them to real exposure: hours actually spent out, with how risky each activity is, so the rate can't drift as habits change.
3. **Re-check births and child survival** after 1. Only tune them if they're still off.
4. **More reasons for couples to part.**
5. **Wolf packs deciding whether to attack**, and your call on discovery pacing.
6. **Speed:** run worlds in parallel properly, and parallelise bands.

Should I start with step 1?
