The 12 worlds are running, around year 4 of 60 so far. I'll report when they're done. Three findings so far, from the code and the earlier runs:

1. **The "too much starvation → no starvation" swing.** The old starvation was mostly one bug: people with empty hearths kept choosing to eat. Before that was found, several buffers were added against what looked like a famine:
   - animal densities raised 30–70×;
   - bigger fat reserves;
   - milk that doesn't fail when the mother is underfed;
   - starvation damage only while the stomach is empty;
   - hunters willing to take more risks when desperate;
   - more sharing.

   After the bug fix, only the plant setting was walked back. So yes: the earlier changes very likely caused this, stacked on each other.
2. **Off-map game subsidy.** Hunting draws on herds from a region 4–8× the map's size, which breed off-map and restock the map every year. That's roughly enough game for about 1,200 people.
3. **Stacked bonuses on nuts and grain.** Skill, interest, lore and three processing discoveries all multiply together. An expert nut gatherer gets about 6.5 rations an hour, around 16,000 kcal/hour. Real figures are 1,000–2,000 kcal/hour, because the gathering numbers ignore shelling, leaching and grinding time. Nuts and grain also store through winter, so this one bonus removes the lean season.

**Your nutrition idea fits here.** Right now all food is one currency, "rations". In reality, lean meat can only be about 35–40% of what people eat; past that you get protein poisoning, also known as "rabbit starvation". Late-winter game is especially lean. So a band living mostly on hunting would really go hungry without fat or plant food. That would be a grounded, emergent brake on exactly the hunting surplus we're seeing: food would come in three kinds instead of one, and real body limits would do the rest.

When the runs finish, I'll have per-world numbers for:
- returns per hour by activity, against real kcal/hour;
- how many hours a day adults spend getting food;
- kills by species, and whether the map's animals are depleted or kept topped up from off-map;
- how fed people are in each season;
- which food discoveries spread.

### Claude (Assistant)

Speed comes right after the food fix. The worlds are at years 36–56 now. With 12 running at once the CPU sits around 58%, so we're finally using the laptop for testing.

**Why each world is slow:**
- **Everyone is updated every simulated hour,** 8,760 times a year, even while asleep or partway through a long walk.
- **Some costs grow faster than the population.** Conversations within a band, meetings between every pair of bands, and lookups like "is this person in camp?" that scan a whole list. That's why 200 people runs at ~3.5 s a year but 1,000 is far slower than 5×.
- **A whole world runs on one core.** Only plant regrowth is spread across cores.

**What I'd do, in order:**
1. **Profile at ~1,000 people.** Find what actually dominates at big sizes rather than guessing.
2. **Cheap fixes first.** Swap list scans for fast lookups (who's in camp, finding a nursing mother) and cut the throwaway temporary objects the hottest code creates every hour. Probably 1.5–3×, with no change to the sim's behaviour.
3. **Skip idle hours.** Someone sleeping or partway through a 6-hour walk doesn't need checking every hour; only wake them when something could change their mind. Possibly the biggest win, but it touches a lot of code, so results need re-checking afterwards.
4. **Bands in parallel.** Bands mostly act on their own, but they share land, animals and meetings. That needs care to keep runs repeatable: same seed, same history. That's the way to use all 24 threads on a single world, and the biggest engineering job here.

**Why food first:** the food fix should bring populations back to real densities. That makes every run cheaper by itself, and it means we profile the sizes the game will actually run at. Speeding up first would partly be speeding up an unrealistic 1,000-person world.

The food runs should finish in about 10–15 minutes. Then I'll bring you the food findings and a proposed fix, and do speed next unless you'd rather swap the order.

The food audit is done: 12 worlds, 60 years each. The excess is real, and it's clear where it comes from.

## What the numbers show

**1. Returns per hour are realistic.** My worry about stacked nut bonuses didn't show up in practice.

| Activity | Sim (rations/hour) | ≈ kcal/hour | Real foragers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Foraging | 0.4–0.6 | 1,000–1,500 | 1,000–3,000 ✅ |
| Hunting and trapping | ~0.33 | ~800 | 500–1,500 ✅ |
| Fishing | 0.35–0.5 | 900–1,250 | ✅ |
| Hours an adult spends getting food per day | 4–6.5 | | ~4–6 ✅ |

The stacking of bonuses in the code is real, but gathering is a minor food source late on, so it isn't the cause. Low priority.

**2. Off-map herds are the main leak, confirmed in all 12 worlds.**
- Hunting drove the map's deer from about 2,300 down to 0–500.
- Yet hunters still kill **about 3,000 deer a year.** Almost every one is a fresh arrival from the off-map herd of about 32,000, which barely shrinks.
- The off-map herd regrows about 2,700 a year, which matches the kills almost exactly.
- So the map's people are living off a region 6.7× bigger than their world. Hunting covers 70–110% of everyone's needs, and diets end up about 75% meat.

Without that restocking, the map's own deer are already hunted out, and hunting would collapse.

**3. The body can't feel mild shortage.** How well fed someone is reads a flat 1.0 whenever their fat is above 40% of what they can carry. Even the hungriest 10% read 1.00 in winter. Fertility and fighting off illness both depend on that number, so a plump woman and a lean one look identical. The birth brake only engages near actual starvation. Real women's fertility responds across the whole range: weight lost in a lean season measurably lowers conception.

**4. Food safety: lots of waste, no sickness from it.** 30–90% as much food spoils as gets eaten, because a big kill brings in more meat than people can eat before it rots. Yet almost nobody gets sick from spoiled meat.

**5. Discoveries: their effects are modest, but they all spread everywhere.** Hunting returns per hour barely rose with new weapons. The hunting boom around years 20–40 is people learning that hunting pays, fed by the endless herds. But by year 60, about 40 discoveries are known by about 85% of everyone in every band, essentially the whole map. Bands end up with no technological differences. That's a discovery-pacing and spread question for later.

**6. Why we swung from starvation to plenty.** The old famines were mostly the eating bug, and the buffers added against them were never removed: denser animals, the off-map pool, bigger fat reserves, milk that holds up, starvation only on an empty stomach. Fixing the bug removed the fake famine but left every buffer in place.

## Proposed fixes, in order (nothing changed yet)

1. **Closed world for animals.** The map is the whole region: no breeding reserve off the edge. Herds still migrate between summer and winter ranges, but within the map. Hunting would then really deplete game, and bands would have to switch to plants and fish or move.
2. **Nutrition, which was your idea.** Split food into lean meat, fat and plant food. Real limits:
   - lean meat can only be about 35–40% of what people live on (more is "rabbit starvation");
   - game is lean in late winter;
   - fat comes from marrow and grease, fish and nuts.

   This puts real pressure back on the lean season and makes plants and fish matter. A diet of mostly lean deer would no longer keep people alive. It also opens new grounded discoveries, such as boiling bones for grease.
3. **A body that feels being lean.** Fertility and immune strength respond to fat stores and to whether people are gaining or losing weight, across the whole range, following real research on women's fertility and energy balance. A lean winter then lowers births without anyone starving.
4. **Then run long** (120+ years, many worlds at once, results saved as they go) and check whether the map levels off at real densities. Only after that, re-check births and child survival.
5. **Smaller items:** cap the stacking of foraging bonuses, and add sickness risk from eating spoiled meat.

1 and 3 are fairly contained. 2 is the bigger design change: it touches every food source, every store and eating itself. I'd do 1 → 3 → test → 2. Or 2 first if you'd rather fix the food model before tuning anything. Want me to start, and in which order? Speed comes right after.

## 1. Is the map big enough?

**For most animals, yes.** The map is 40 × 40 km.

| Animal | Real range | Fits on the map? |
|---|---|---|
| Red deer | 5–50 km² a year; seasonal moves (valley to hills) of 10–30 km | ✅ |
| Boar, hares, wolves | resident | ✅ |
| Mammoths, and reindeer if we ever add them | hundreds of km | ❌ a 40 km map can't hold a herd's real range |

A real band used about 300–1,000 km², so the map holds a handful of bands' worth of country. That's right for the population sizes we want.

**What's wrong is that the off-map land is empty of people.** Herds really do wander in from outside, but the land outside would have hunters too. The fair version is that animals cross the edge in both directions, and the land beyond is assumed to be hunted as hard as the map is. Then hunting the map out just leaves both sides thin. For mammoths, the pool can stay, but it should be thinned by hunting off the map as well. So I'd revise fix 1: animals move across the edges based on how crowded each side is, and the outside is never a free, unhunted refill.

**Weather is the bigger hole you've found.** There's a random swing in temperature each year (hard winters are colder), but nothing else varies:
- plant yields are identical every year;
- animal birth rates never change;
- there are no failed nut years, droughts, deep-snow winters that kill deer, or poor fish runs.

That matters a lot. Real forager populations weren't held flat by constant mild hunger. They grew in good years and crashed in bad ones, a few times a generation, and the average came out near zero growth. Our worlds never get a bad year, so they only ever grow. Making years vary is grounded and fully emergent: no event scripts, just a random year's conditions feeding into plant and animal growth.

## 2. How real is rabbit starvation?

The core fact is solid:
- the liver can only process about 250–300 g of protein a day, so lean meat can't be more than about 35–40% of what someone lives on;
- explorers and trappers who lived on lean game in late winter got sick and weak while eating plenty;
- archaeologist John Speth's work shows foragers chasing fat, throwing away lean meat in spring, and boiling bones for grease.

**But it's weaker than I made it sound.** A whole deer isn't pure lean: there's marrow, organs, brain, and in autumn real body fat. It only bites:
- in late winter and spring, when animals are thin;
- when there's little fish, nuts or other fat to go with the meat.

So it's a real seasonal pressure, not a constant cap. It's worth doing in a simple form, as a fat share for each kill that depends on season and species, rather than a full nutrition system. It's also not the main cause of the surplus, so it can wait until after 1, 3 and weather.

## 3, 4 and 5

Agreed.

## Revised order

1. **Animals cross the map edge both ways,** with the land outside assumed to be hunted as hard as the map. No free refill.
2. **Variable years:**
   - good and bad mast, berry and grain years;
   - fish runs that vary;
   - hard winters that thin the deer and boar;
   - years tied together, so bad years can come in runs.
3. **A body that feels being lean:** fertility and immune strength respond to fat stores across the whole range.
4. **Long runs:** 120+ years, many worlds at once, results saved as they go. Check whether the population levels off and whether booms and crashes look real.
5. **Fat share of game by season** (the simple rabbit-starvation version), the cap on stacking foraging bonuses, and sickness from spoiled meat.
6. **Speed.**

Should I start on 1–3?

### Claude (Assistant)

## Weather within the map

Yes, that's what drives migration. In the sim, deer move between summer and winter ground on a fixed calendar. In reality they move because conditions differ across the land:
- high ground gets deep snow and greens up late, so deer winter in the valleys and follow the green-up uphill in spring;
- one valley has a dry year while the next is lush, and herds drift toward the better grazing.

The map already has elevation, so this can come out of real causes:
- temperature drops with height (about 6.5 °C per km);
- snow lies deeper on high ground;
- spring arrives later on hills;
- rain varies from region to region, differently each year.

Plants grow according to their local conditions, and animals go where food is good and snow is shallow. Migration then happens because of the land, not a calendar, and people have to follow the herds or switch foods.

## Off-map: open world, no simulated outside hunting

Agreed. Herds coming and going across the edge is how people experienced it. We're not an island.

On your question "shouldn't hunting shrink the herd?" It does shrink: the off-map deer fell from about 39,000 to about 32,000. Then it stops shrinking, because a thinned herd has more food per animal and breeds faster. That's real ecology, and it's why real hunting rarely wiped out common deer. The herd settles where the births match the kills.

So the breeding isn't the problem. These are:
1. **The off-map herd is huge,** 6.7× the map's deer, so it can absorb 3,000 kills a year and barely notice.
2. **The map is refilled every year.** Arrivals top the map back up to its "fair share" of the region's herd, a full restock each spring. Real animals move in from outside much more slowly, mostly young animals spreading out. So in practice the map's people are hunting the whole region.
3. **Migratory animals are huntable all year.** You're right that they shouldn't be. A migrating herd should be on the map for a season and then gone. That's what made real people's lives seasonal: big hunts when the herds pass through, other food the rest of the year.

## Proposed animal changes
- **Resident animals** (boar, hares, wolves, and the deer that don't migrate) live and breed on the map. If you hunt them out, they recover only slowly, as young animals drift in from the edges.
- **Migratory herds** come and go across the edge when conditions draw them: snow, green-up, grazing. They're only on the map for part of the year. The off-map herd breeds without any hunting off the map. How many come through, and when, depends on conditions and on how dangerous the map has proved, which is already partly built.
- **Year-to-year variation:** good and bad mast years, fish runs and winters, layered on top of the differences across the map.

Plus **the body that feels being lean**, which you're on board with.

## Order
1. Weather that varies across the map (temperature with height, snow, green-up, regional rain), with plants responding.
2. Animals move toward conditions: migration emerges, migratory herds are only there part of the year, residents don't get refilled.
3. Years that vary: masts, runs, winters.
4. A body that feels being lean.
5. Long runs across many worlds to check whether the population levels off and booms and busts look real.

Then the smaller items, then speed. I'll check in with results after step 2, since that's the biggest change to how the world behaves. Should I start?

### Claude (Assistant)

Progress update. Steps 1 and 2 are built and tested on their own, and step 3 is written. The 12-world test with people on the new ecology is running (years 5–11 of 60), and I'll report when it finishes.

**Done:**
- **Weather across the map:**
  - temperature falls with height, so the hills are about 5 °C colder;
  - snow builds and settles, giving 30–50 cm in an average winter (deeper on the hills, lighter under trees);
  - each year brings a rain pattern by district, plus good and bad nut masts, berry and grain years, and fish runs.

  Plants grow by local warmth and rain, foraging gets harder under snow, and people feel the weather at their own camp's height.
- **Closed herds:** no off-map reserve and no restocking. Each herd has a body condition from grazing where it stands. Hard winters kill thin animals, births follow condition and crowding, and herds move to better ground. The no-people ecology test across 6 worlds over 50 years:
  - deer swing between about 2,000 and 6,000 and stay on the map, moving down to the valley floor in winter;
  - hares boom and crash;
  - boar track the nut masts;
  - mammoths spend summers on the map and winter away (same animals, nobody hunts them there);
  - wolves hold at 5–40, finding prey by scent within a few km and turning to other prey when deer are scarce.
- **A body that feels being lean:** fertility follows fat and whether someone's gaining or losing weight, and immune strength weakens with real undernutrition. This isn't in the current test run; it comes next, so we can see each change's effect separately.
- **Fixed along the way:** hare meat 2 rations → 1 (a hare is about 2 kg of meat).

**Hand-set numbers to disclose:**
- the elevation scale (ridges about 800 m up);
- the animals' feeding needs and winter-forage level;
- the 4 km scent range for wolves.

The animals' birth and death rates are from field figures.

**A question for you:** in one of the six no-people worlds, the wolves died out by year 14. That's real for an isolated population of about 40, but on a closed map they never come back. Should wolves (and other locally extinct species) occasionally wander in from beyond the map, or is extinction permanent?

### Claude (Assistant)

### Claude (Assistant)

## Extinction

Permanent, then. One consequence worth knowing: if the wolves die out, no band can domesticate dogs from then on, because the first dog step needs a wolf pack nearby. That's a nice emergent result.

## Dog domestication

It's there, as four discoveries in a chain:

| Stage | What it represents | What it does in the sim now |
|---|---|---|
| `dog.lurk` | Wolves hanging around camp for scraps (needs wolves within ~5 km) | Wolves are 1.3× more wary of attacking the camp |
| `dog.pup` | A pup raised in camp (needs wolves around and someone interested in animals) | 1.1× more |
| `dog.camp` | Camp dogs, kept over generations | 1.5× more, and the camp is likelier to wake to a night raid |
| `dog.hunt` | Hunting dogs | Hunting skill +0.2 |

**The weakness:** dogs are only knowledge, not animals. A band that "knows" camp dogs keeps them forever without feeding them, and the dogs never breed or die. Real dogs were animals people had to feed, and they could be lost in a famine. A proper version would give each band a dog pack that eats scraps and meat, breeds, guards the camp, helps hunts and carries loads, and dies in hard times. That fits the closed-world ecology we just built. I'd put it on the list after this food pass.

## How much discoveries mattered

So far the evidence says not much, and not in a good way.

1. **Returns per hour barely moved.** In the 12-world food audit, hunting stayed around 0.33 rations an hour and foraging 0.4–0.6 from year 10 to year 60, even as bows, traps, nut processing and drying spread. The hunting boom around years 20–40 came from people learning that hunting pays, on top of the endless off-map herds, not from new weapons.
2. **Everything spread everywhere.** By year 60, about 40 discoveries were known by about 85% of everyone in every band. That leaves no technological differences between bands, and no band having something another lacks, which takes away much of what makes discoveries interesting.
3. **Earlier one-at-a-time tests** (handing a discovery to everyone and comparing):
   - throwing spears cut starvation from 47% to 32% of deaths;
   - cooking and food preservation made little difference to population (about 280 vs 285 people).

   But those were measured back when the eating bug distorted everything.
4. **82 of the 103 stages do something directly in the code.** That's coverage, not impact: many effects are 1.1× or 1.15× multipliers that disappear into the noise.

**My read:** with food unlimited, discoveries couldn't matter. Nobody was short, so a better spear or a drying rack changed nothing. Once food is really limited by closed herds, lean winters, masts and runs, discoveries should start to count, because a band that dries fish or boils bone grease gets through a winter that another band doesn't.

**What I'd do:** once the food changes settle, run proper with-and-without experiments across many worlds:
- discovery on vs off;
- each key discovery handed to every band vs to none.

That measures each one's real effect on survival and population. Separately, look at why ideas spread so fast between bands; it's probably the visits and talk passing on too freely. Both feed your discovery-pacing decision.

The 12-world run with people is still going. I'll bring the food and population results when it finishes, then turn on the leanness change for a comparison run.

The run with all three changes has started; it'll take roughly 30 minutes. Here's what the previous run showed, with only the new ecology and none of the behaviour fixes.

**Ecology only (12 worlds, 60 years):**
- Populations stayed small and roughly flat: 31–180 people, against 300–450 and climbing before. Food is finally the limit.
- There were real hungry periods: a few worlds had 11–16 starvation deaths in a bad year, then recovered. That's the kind of hard but survivable life you described.
- But people **hunted the deer and boar to extinction in about half the worlds** within 30–50 years, killing 10–18 deer per person per year and letting most of it rot. The wolves then starved, and people lived mostly off fish.

**What's in the current run:**
1. The new ecology, as before.
2. The body that feels being lean: fertility drops in lean seasons.
3. The spoilage fix: food is only worth what will be eaten before it spoils. With fresh food at home or meat around camp, people stop going out for more.

What I'm looking for:
- deer and boar persist alongside people;
- populations stay marginal (surviving, not thriving);
- births fall in lean years;
- hunting takes a realistic few animals per person a year.

### Claude (Assistant)

Saved. Here's what that framing means in practice.

**The map as a whole:** humans should persist across nearly all worlds, with near-zero long-run growth, real lean years and the occasional hard decade. The last few runs are close to that already.

**Single bands:** dying out is normal. A band of 30 hit by a bad winter, a sickness, a failed hunt season or a raid can end, and that's realistic. What matters is that the map isn't emptied. So I'll track two separate numbers: bands lost (expected) and whole worlds lost (should be rare).

**Discovery is where your reframe changes the most.** Right now about 40 discoveries spread to nearly everyone by year 60. Under your target that's far too fast. Without a god, a world should gain only a handful of new things in 60 years, unevenly, with some bands even losing knowledge when the last person who knew it dies. That means:
- the discovery rate should probably come down;
- ideas should spread between bands more slowly; right now they flow too freely through visits and talk.

**What the god layer is for:** speeding up innovation, writing story and drama, rescuing people, and generally improving their odds. All of those only matter against a slow, fragile baseline, so this calibration sets up the god layer well.

When the current run finishes, I'll report against these measures:
- the map's people persisting;
- band extinctions;
- growth near zero;
- how many discoveries appear and how far they spread.

That tells us whether discovery pacing is next.

### Claude (Assistant)

### Claude (Assistant)

Hunting is fixed. In the last test, 12 worlds over 60 years, **deer, boar and wolves survived alongside people in all 12**. The worst remaining problem is new: lean winters starve too many people. All 12 tests pass, and the full log is in `docs/PROGRESS.md`.

## How hunting was fixed

It took nine rounds of test runs, because there were five separate causes. Each fix uses a real mechanism rather than a tuned number:

1. **Food work no longer looks worth doing when food is piled up.** Extra food is only worth what can be eaten before it spoils, and that now counts all food in camp.
2. **Hunting parties count meat already in camp** when judging whether a kill is worth it.
3. **The meat craving follows diet.** People crave meat when it's been under about 10% of what their family eats, and stop craving it around 40%. Before, the craving was nearly always on, because warm-weather meat rots in four days.
4. **Searching for game takes real time.** A party that finds nothing walks on for hours, so when game is scarce whole days come back empty and people switch to other food.
5. **Hunted herds learn to be shy of people:** heavily hunted animals keep their distance for a year or two.

The no-people test of the land also exposed five problems in the animals themselves, now fixed:
- **Wolf kills overlapped natural deaths.** Wolves mostly take animals that were about to die anyway, but here every kill was extra.
- **Wolves always went for the meatiest prey.** Now they take what they come across, which gives a scarce prey room to recover.
- **Wolves wasted carcasses.** They now stay and feed on one for days.
- **Deer conceived at the wrong time.** Pregnancy is now set by condition at the autumn rut, not by the thin spring condition.
- **Accidents were 4× too common.** Falls and drownings are now about 5% of deaths, as among real foragers.

## Where the worlds stand

| | Sim | Real foragers |
|---|---|---|
| Deer / boar / wolves | 5,000–6,000 / ~2,700 / 29–40, in all 12 worlds | stable |
| People per world at year 60 | 25–193 (one world dwindling) | 80–480 on this map |
| Growth | about 0–1.3% a year | about 0 |
| Infant deaths | 17–33% | 15–25% |
| Reaching age 15 | 41–75% | 55–65% |
| Births per woman | 5.4–7.2 | 4.5–6.2 |
| Accidents | ~5% of deaths | 5–10% |
| **Starvation** | **20–55% of deaths** | a minor cause |

Overall this matches your target: people survive without thriving, and one world fading is fine.

## What still needs your input

**1. Uneven famine (the big one).** Most of the year everyone is fully fed. But in lean winters and springs, the hungriest tenth of people drop to near-empty while the band's average is still 0.75–0.85, and adults in their prime starve along with small children. Real foragers survived lean seasons in four ways:
- **Sharing widely in a crisis.** Here people refuse too readily, or the hungry don't ask.
- **Fallback foods:** inner bark, roots dug under snow, fish through the ice. Here winter plant food is close to zero.
- **Winter stores:** drying and caching. These exist but are probably too weak.
- **Moving camp** to better ground.

I'd investigate which of these is failing before changing anything. Do you want me to start there?

**2. Births are still high** at 5.4–7.2 per woman. It's probably because people are fully fed most of the year, so the new link between leanness and fertility rarely engages. I'd re-check after the famine fix.

**3. Discovery spread.** Ideas pass in one lucky conversation, so crafts spread like gossip. My proposal is that crafts need repeated watching and practice, while hearing about something only makes reinventing it more likely. Combined with slower discovery, that fits your "advance slowly without a god" target.

**Also still open:** couples rarely break up, dogs are knowledge only rather than animals, and speed.

### Claude (Assistant)

A likely cause is already visible. A household only counts food beyond **two days' worth** as spare. So in a lean season, when everyone is down to a day or two, nobody has anything to give, even to a family with nothing at all. Real foragers do the opposite in hard times: food gets levelled across families, especially meat and especially for children. The death logs should confirm whether the starving people had empty hearths while neighbours held a day or two.

### Claude (Assistant)

The picture at year 45 in one world: deer were holding at about 5,000 while people took 600–970 a year (about 5 per person). Then a hard winter starved about 1,200 deer, wolves took about 870, people kept hunting, and the herd collapsed.

I've made a lot of changes since your last message, and this has become a design decision rather than a bug. Here's where things stand.

## Item 1, famine: the structural fixes worked
- **Sharing in a crisis:** families with food now share down to the hungry family's level, as foragers' demand sharing does, instead of keeping everything under 2 days' worth.
- **No more microbands:** hard times no longer splinter bands into groups of 2–4. Small groups join bands they know, and splits are now 6–14 people.
- **Inner bark** as a lean-season fallback food. It's slow to gather, and the woods near camp get stripped.
- **Result:** starvation fell from about 38% of deaths to about 15% over the first 30 years.

## The new problem: nothing slows growth until famine and game collapse
With winter survivable, populations grow about 2% a year, reaching 170–350 by year 60. Births per woman are about 6–7, Hadza-like; the real-world fertility curve and secondary sterility I added barely changed that.

The only brakes come late and hard:
- **famine** again, at 25–67% of deaths in years 30–60;
- **deer extinction** in 7 of 12 worlds.

The arithmetic: this map's game can sustainably feed about 110 people eating only meat, or about 220 eating half meat, after the wolves' share. Once people pass that, the deer go. Real foragers at these densities (0.1–0.2 per km²) did live alongside deer. They had more kinds of game (elk, aurochs, beaver, birds) and more fish, and when hunting got poor they ate more plants or moved on, long before the game was gone.

## Options
- **A. Richer real fauna.** Add more species of the right kind for a temperate valley: red and roe deer, aurochs or bison, beaver, waterfowl, and stronger fish runs. More total game, spread across prey with different habits. Grounded, but a bigger job.
- **B. Hunting effort that falls with scarcity.** Hunters currently share every sighting through gossip, so the last herds still get found. Sightings could go stale faster, or tell less. Smaller and more targeted.
- **C. Births responding before famine.** Fertility currently only drops once people are thin. Real women's fertility also falls under steady hard work and long foraging days, which would slow growth before famine hits.
- **D. Accept it.** Bands boom, overshoot, and crash with famine and game loss. That's arguably how many real populations behaved, and it's very much "survive, not thrive". But losing all the deer in more than half of worlds seems too much.

**My recommendation is B + C first** (smaller and grounded), then A later as an ecology upgrade.

Four 150-year worlds are running in the background to show where the ceiling actually ends up; I'll share them when they finish. Which way do you want to go?

### Claude (Assistant)

---

### Claude (Assistant)

Taking these in order.

## 1. Splitting
I agree. The point of a size limit is that bigger bands should need better technology. That's how it worked for real foragers: a band of about 25 is roughly what can feed itself within a day's walk of camp, and bigger groups needed things like storage, fish weirs, wild grain, or ways to keep peace among many people.

That isn't what drives splits right now. Most splits are **"bad blood"**: grudges pile up and a band breaks apart every few years whatever its size. In one 20-year run there were 65 splits and 400+ family moves, with bands averaging 10–14 people. So neither the player nor the bands learn "we need technology to grow"; they just churn.

What I'd change, all emergent:
- **Splits come from what size really costs.** As a band grows, the land near camp gets stripped, so people walk farther for less. That pressure builds and pushes a split. Better technology (storage, fishing gear, grain, dogs) raises the size a camp can feed.
- **Big groups mean more friction,** since people know fewer of the others. Shared customs and rituals already ease this in the code. Leadership or ritual discoveries would ease it more, which is the path toward villages and towns.
- **Grudges cause fewer whole-band splits.** Real foragers mostly resolved feuds by one family moving to another band, not by splitting.

## 2. Inner bark
It's real. Scots pine inner bark was ground into famine bread in Finland and Sweden into the 1800s. The Mohawk name for the Adirondack people means "bark eaters". Archaeologists find bark-peeled pines thousands of years old in Scandinavia and British Columbia. But it was a famine food, and you don't have to like it.

It also didn't work out: by getting people through winter, it removed the brake on growth. I'd **take it out**, or at most make it a discovery. That fits your "tech lets bands grow" idea: a band that learns it gets a famine buffer.

## 3. Nobody eats only meat
That was just my arithmetic: the game on this map could feed about 110 people if they ate nothing else. In the runs, hunting supplies about 20–60% of food and plants the rest.

## 4. Hunting: is it a real tracking system?
Mostly, yes:
- Every herd has a **real position** and moves each day by its own choices.
- Each person keeps their **own last-seen sighting** of herds they've personally seen within about 1 km, or heard about.
- A hunt heads to the **believed location** and has to **track** from there. The chance of catching up falls with how far the herd has moved since it was seen, and rises with skill and dogs. Following the trail closes the gap. A party with no sightings now roams and searches.

Its weak spots:
- **Gossip passes exact coordinates.** Sightings spread through camp talk, so everyone knows where every herd was seen in the last 3 days. That's what lets people find the last few herds.
- **There's a small knowledge leak:** a party's list of targets silently drops herds that have died or left the map, so people "know" things they couldn't.
- **Tracks aren't physical.** There are no trails or footprints left on the land, and snow doesn't help with tracking (in reality fresh snow makes it far easier). Tracking is just a dice roll on distance and skill.

A proper version would have herds leave **tracks on the cells they cross**, fading over days, much faster in rain and lasting longer in snow. Hunters would find and follow fresh tracks, and second-hand news would be vaguer ("deer by the north marsh a few days ago"), not exact coordinates. That's real tracking, and it naturally makes scarce game hard to find.

## Proposed order
1. Remove inner bark, or make it a discovery.
2. Rework splitting around what size costs, plus friction among many people, with technology raising the limit.
3. Physical tracks and vaguer sighting news, which should also stop the deer wipeouts.
4. Then re-check famine, births and growth.

Do you want bark removed, or made a discovery?

### Claude (Assistant)

Then I'll bring it back as a true desperation food, set so it can't become a staple:
- **Only when actually short:** only someone whose family is nearly out of food, and who is getting thin, will strip bark. Nobody does it in ordinary times.
- **Poor returns:** about 0.12 rations an hour for an average forager, roughly 300 kcal. That's about half what I had before, since peeling, drying and pounding take most of the effort. A day of it keeps someone alive but doesn't feed a family.
- **Depleting:** about 10 days' worth per patch of woods, regrowing over decades, so the woods near a camp get stripped and it stays a short-term buffer.

That way it might save a few lives in the worst late winters without lifting the ceiling. I'll add it after the current test so we can see its effect separately. The tracking and splitting test is about halfway to the year-20 checkpoint.

The year-30 checkpoint, across 12 worlds:

| | Now (v16) | Before this round |

| Tiny bands at a time | 0.6–3 per world | 3–5 |
| Family moves over 30 years | 65–270 | 240–660 over 20 years |

| Population trend | mixed: 4 growing, 4 flat, 4 shrinking (one down to 16) | mostly shrinking |

## What's done from the plan
1. **Bark:** removed, then brought back as true desperation food: only for people from nearly empty hearths who are getting thin, about 300 kcal an hour, and the nearby woods get stripped.
2. **Splitting:** bands now split over what size costs them: long working days as the land near camp is stripped, crowding, and hard times. A personal feud sends one family to another band instead of splitting the whole band. Groups too small to stand alone don't found new bands. Splits now happen at about 25–35 people, so technology that shortens the working day should let bands grow larger.
3. **Tracking:**
   - herds leave week-long trails;
   - tracks stay readable for about 2 days on dry ground, 3 in mud and 6 in snow;
   - hunters follow the trail from the last sighting or lose it and must search;
   - people notice fresh tracks as they move about;
   - second-hand news is blurred by about a kilometre.

   This is what finally stopped the deer wipeouts.
4. **Re-check:** famine is still the weak point.

## What's left of famine (item 1)
It's now clearly a **spring gap**. Winter stores are almost entirely nuts, so they rise and fall with the mast:
- after a big mast year, bands store 3,000–5,000 rations and sail through to summer;
- after a poor mast year they store 20–1,300, and the following spring is a famine.

Real temperate foragers hedged this with dried meat and fish from autumn. That needs a discovery here, `dry.keep`. I've just made bands that know it hunt and fish hard in autumn to stock up. That's in this run, but I don't have its full results yet.

So without drying, bands hit famine every few years, and some dwindle. That fits your target that people don't thrive without technology and that small bands sometimes die out. But starvation at about 24% of deaths is still high: in reality, hunger usually killed by making people sick rather than through outright starvation.

## Next
- Look at the full 60-year results from this run, especially whether bands that discover drying stabilise. That would be exactly the "technology lets you grow" behaviour you want.
- Then **2. births**, **3. discovery spread** (slow and uneven without a god), **4. breakups**, **5. dogs as animals**, **6. speed**.

### Claude (Assistant)

Here's what's happening, using world 3 as the example:
- **Years 40–51:** growth from 158 to 232 with almost no starvation; everyone fed at 0.97–0.99.
- **Year 52:** a bad spring hits a population at its peak, and 30 people starve.
- **After that:** it levels off around 210, with smaller hungry springs every few years.

So the land does now impose a ceiling, around 100–230 people per world depending on the map. But **famine is the only thing enforcing it**, so starvation dominates the death records once a world reaches its ceiling. Drying food raises the ceiling, the "technology lets bands grow" effect you wanted, but each world then grows into the new ceiling and famine returns.

In real foragers near their ceiling, other brakes engaged before outright famine:
- **Fewer births.** Women's fertility falls with steady hard work and seasonal weight loss; here it only falls once women are already thin.
- **More disease.** Sickness spreads faster in bigger, more crowded camps, and hunger makes people sicker. In historical famines, most deaths are recorded as disease rather than starvation.
- **Moving away.** Families left for less crowded country.

## Where everything stands

| Item | State |
|---|---|
| **1. Famine** | Partly fixed. No more microbands or band-hopping. Sharing in a crisis, desperation bark and autumn stocking-up are in. Remaining: famine is the only ceiling. |
| Tracking | Done: real trails, tracks that fade, blurred second-hand news. |
| Splitting | Done: driven by long working days and crowding; feuds move one family instead. |
| Deer | Persist in 9 of 12 worlds over 60 years (3 lost at the largest populations). |
| **2. Births** | 5–7.6 per woman. Ties into the ceiling question above. |
| **3. Discovery spread** | Still too uniform: drying is known by 79–97% everywhere. |
| **4. Breakups / 5. Dogs / 6. Speed** | Not started. |

## The decision I need from you
Should the ceiling come mainly from:
- **(a) famine,** with booms and busts: harsh, and starvation dominates deaths;
- **(b) or gradual brakes first:** fertility responding to workload and seasonal weight loss, and disease spreading more in crowded camps, so famine becomes the occasional bad year rather than the main regulator?

I recommend (b). It's closer to the evidence, and it fits "bands survive but don't thrive". After that I'd move to births (which (b) mostly covers), then discovery spread, breakups, dogs and speed.

### Claude (Assistant)

## How the autumn stocking-up works

People carry a learned sense of **how lean the winters are**, built from how hungry they actually were in past winters and springs and spread through talk. From late summer through autumn, each person compares their family's **keeping food** (nuts, grain, dried food) with how much they expect the lean months to need. If it falls short, they feel an urge to put food by. I call this "foresight".

**Before:** foresight only pushed people toward foraging for nuts and grain, which keep, plus a little fishing.

**What I added**, only for people who know how to dry food (`dry.keep`):
- foresight also pushes them to **hunt** in autumn, while game is fattest, and to **fish** the autumn runs;
- in autumn they count meat as keeping for about 60 days, since they'll dry it, instead of "it rots in 4 days, so a big kill is wasted";
- surplus fresh food then gets dried into stores that last months.

**The side effect:** in the biggest worlds, that autumn hunting pushed deer over the edge in 3 of 12. The tracking makes scarce deer hard to find, but large populations hunting hard every autumn still added up. I'll watch for this after option (b).

## Starting on (b): brakes before famine

1. **Births respond to hard work, not just thinness.** Studies of farming and foraging women (Ellison, Jasienska) show heavy daily work lowers ovarian hormones and conception, even when women aren't thin. Long food-work days should therefore lower fertility before famine hits. The sim now tracks each person's working hours, so this can plug straight in.
2. **Disease spreads more in crowded camps.** Checking how contagion works now.

### Claude (Assistant)

Honest answers on both.

## Did "technology → bigger bands" work?
**Not tested yet, and I can't claim it works.** Bands now split around 25–35 people, mostly from long working days, so in principle technology that cuts the working day should let bands grow. I haven't run the comparison that would prove it: the same worlds with and without key food technologies, checking whether bands get bigger.

**Volume is still too high.** Recent runs show 4–30 splits per world over 30 years, and 4–12 bands on the map at a time. That's better than before (65 splits in 20 years) but still too many for a player.

## Thinking about it as a player
You're right: following 10 constantly shifting bands would be miserable, especially in v1. Real anthropology suggests a way out.

Real foragers had **two levels**:
- a **people** of a few hundred who shared a language, name and customs, intermarried and visited;
- **camps** of 15–40 within that people, which split and rejoined constantly with the seasons and with quarrels. Families drifted between camps without becoming different people.

Right now the sim treats every camp split as founding a whole new named band with its own identity. That's what creates the volume.

**My proposal:**
- **The player's unit is the people,** for example "the Tor". It's one identity with a shared culture, chronicle and relationship to the god, made up of 1–4 camps.
- **Camps split and rejoin freely within the people,** like real fission-fusion, without spawning new peoples. To the player, that reads as "the Tor wintered in three camps this year".
- **A true schism, a group breaking away to become a new people, is rare and dramatic:** a deep feud, a long famine, or a charismatic leader. It's a story beat, perhaps once in a few generations, something the god might even provoke or prevent.
- **The map holds 3–5 peoples in v1:** the player's own, which they work with closely, plus a few neighbours who matter less (trade, marriages, raids, rivals).
- **Technology raises how many people a camp can hold.** Storage, better hunting and fishing gear, and grain let camps grow toward villages, and the people grows into a town over time. That gives your "tech → bigger groups" path a visible payoff.

This is a substantial restructure of how bands are represented. All the behaviour underneath (moving, sharing, splitting for real reasons) carries over; it just sits inside a two-level structure. It's a big enough design change that I'd like your yes or no before building it.

## Meanwhile
Two test rounds are running: v18 (births, crowd disease, discovery spread) and v19 (adds breakups and dogs). I'll report both. The queue: finish famine and births → this people/camps restructure, if you approve → breakups and dogs check → speed → hard-coding audit.

### Claude (Assistant)

Yes. Here's how the levels would fit together. Nothing in the social system we built gets lost; it just gains a layer above it.

## Four levels of conflict, smallest to largest

| Level | What happens | Frequency | Identity change? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Person to person** | Everything we built: asking, refusing, theft, confronting, fights, grudges, gossip, learned norms, guilt, trust. | Constant | None |
| **2. A family leaves a camp** | The forager way to end a feud or escape a bad situation: move to another camp of your people, usually where kin live. | Common | None, same people |
| **3. A camp splits or merges** | Camps break apart when they're too big for the land around them (long days, crowding, hard times) and come back together when small or in a bad season. | Every few years | None. "The Tor wintered in three camps." |
| **4. A schism** | Part of the people breaks away for good and becomes a new people with its own name. | Rare, a story beat | Yes |

**Conflict between camps:** yes, it can still happen. Grudges are personal and carry across camps, and camps keep grievances against each other just as bands do now. The learned violence rules already treat kin and friends differently from strangers. Camps of one people are full of relatives, so fighting between them should be rarer and more reluctant, without being impossible. A feud between camps that keeps escalating, made worse by the camps drifting apart in customs and living far apart, is exactly what leads to a schism. Splits at level 3 and schisms at level 4 come from different things: level 3 from what size costs, level 4 from deep division.

## Start and ceiling
- **Start with one people:** your people, maybe 3–4 camps and about 100 in total, all sharing a name and customs.
- **Other peoples arise only through schism,** which makes each one an event with history: they broke away from you.
- **The ceiling of 3–5 peoples:** much of it emerges naturally, since schism is rare and this map can only feed a few hundred people. But I'd also add an **explicit cap of 5 as a game rule**, for performance and manageability. I'll label it as a design rule rather than a fact about the world, so it's honest about being authored. Past the cap, the sim stops creating new peoples; a breakaway faction joins another people instead, or stays as an unhappy camp.

## What changes in the code
The existing camp code stays: moving, sharing, hunting, foraging and splitting all keep working at camp level. On top of that:
- a new **People** layer holding the name, the shared customs and the chronicle (the story you'll follow);
- splits make new camps of the same people instead of new named bands;
- joining a camp of your own people is easy, while joining another people is harder;
- a **schism** check driven by grievances between camps, drifting customs and distance.

**Two questions before I build:**
1. **Start as one people of 3–4 camps** (about 100 people), or **one single camp** of about 30 that grows and splits from there? The second makes the opening more personal.
2. **Is the hard cap of 5 peoples OK** as a labelled game rule?

### Claude (Assistant)

---

### Claude (Assistant)

Civil wars can still happen. The fix doesn't forbid violence within a people; it changes which learned expectations people use.

**How it works now:**
- Every person carries learned expectations about what hitting someone brings, separately for kin, friends, neutral acquaintances, people they distrust, and outsiders.
- **Each people starts with its own culture.** Its baseline violence expectation is drawn at random, so some peoples start more violent and others more peaceful, and the gap between how they treat insiders and outsiders also varies.
- **Cultures drift with experience.** If attacks pay off, people learn violence works, and it spreads through watching and gossip. If they backfire, the taboo strengthens.
- Before tonight's fix, everyone in another camp counted as an outsider, so kin camps used the weakest taboo and went to war within a year. Now members of another camp of your people are judged by your real relationship with them.

**So a civil war can grow naturally.** Camps squabble over shared ground, grudges and distrust build, and people come to distrust the other camp. Distrust carries a weaker taboo than kinship or friendship, so violence gets easier and raids can follow. Long bad blood is also the biggest part of what drives a **schism**: the feuding camp breaks away as a new people. Then they really are outsiders to each other, and wars between peoples follow the usual rules. That's a nice emergent arc: feud, civil war, split, rival peoples.

**One thing I did hard-code** and should fix: a raid against a camp of your own people is always weighed as if against "neutral" acquaintances. It should depend on how the raider actually feels about that camp, so a camp they've come to distrust is easier to attack than one full of friends. Fixing it now.

### Claude (Assistant)

**Monogamy is hard-coded right now,** and you're right that it doesn't need to be. Each person has a single partner slot, and pairing only happens between two unpartnered people. Real foragers varied: most marriages were monogamous, some men had two wives (often 5–15% of men in forager societies; the !Kung and Hadza had some), and the practice depended on culture and circumstance. It was commoner where men were scarce, as in our small worlds after raids. Some peoples also had widow inheritance or occasional polyandry.

A non-hard-coded version would let a person take a second partner when both want it. It would be weighed against the existing partner's jealousy (already in the sim as an innate reaction) and against a **learned custom of the people**, which forms from what people actually do, like the existing custom about who moves at marriage. Some peoples would then drift toward allowing plural marriage and others away from it, and a shortage of men would naturally push in that direction. I'll add it to the queue.

**Gender roles are mostly emergent, not hard-coded.** What is built in is biological:
- women carry, give birth and nurse;
- late pregnancy and nursing an infant make a long hunt costly, which steers nursing mothers away from hunting;
- bodies differ by sex on average (strength, build).

What isn't built in: there's no "men hunt, women gather" rule. Who hunts, forages, fishes or cooks comes from each person's strength, interests, skills and situation, so a division of labour emerges from those, as anthropologists think it did. The customs on who moves at marriage and who keeps the children after a breakup are learned from what people actually do. The remaining sex-specific rules are biological: women can only conceive until about 45, men can partner until 60.

**On the lone wanderers:** the chronicle shows strike-outs are whole families (2–6 people), 8 in 15 years, not single men, so they're not what's lowering births. The bigger signal in that world is **68 killings in 15 years**. Violence is cutting into the men, which is what leaves young women unpaired. I'll check whether that's typical across the 12 worlds once the run with the latest fix finishes.
